PREFACE

This Report has been prepared for submission to the Governor under Article
151 ofthe Constitution of India.

Chapter I deals with the findings of performance audit in the Planning,
Economics and Statistics and Water Resources departments, Chapter II deals
with findings of transaction audit in Animal Husbandry; Culture; Forest;
Higher Education; Labour; Medical Education; Narmada Valley
Development; Panchayat and Rural Development; Public Health and Family
Welfare; Public Works; Scheduled Tribes and Scheduled Castes Welfare;
Urban Administration and Development and Water Resources departments.
Chapter III deals with the integrated audit of the Farmers Welfare and
Agriculture Development Department and Chief Controlling Officer based
audit of the Animal Husbandry Department.

Reports containing (a) observations on the finances of the State Government,
(b) observations arising out of audit of Statutory Corporations, Boards and
Government Companies and (c) observations on revenue receipts of the State
Government are being presented separately.

The cases mentioned in this Report are among those which came to notice
during the course of test audit of accounts for 2009-10 as well as those which
had come to notice in earlier years but were not included in previous Reports.
Matters relating to the period subsequent to 2009-10 have also been included,
wherever necessary.

vii


Overview

CS 4 » ५ ५। १, ओिििओ

The Report includes three chapters containing five reviews and 21 paragraphs
dealing with the results of performance audit of selected programmes and schemes as
well as audit of the financial transactions of the Government.

The audit has been conducted in accordance with the Auditing Standards prescribed
for the Indian Audit and Accounts Department. Audit samples have been drawn
based on statistical sampling methods as well as on judgement basis. The specific
audit methodology adopted for programmes and schemes has been mentioned in the
reviews. The audit conclusions have been drawn and recommendations made, taking
into consideration the views of the Government.

Asummary of the important findings is given below:
1. Madhya Pradesh Assembly Constituency Area Development Scheme

The Madhya Pradesh Assembly Constituency Area Development Scheme was
launched in July 1994 with the objective of taking up developmental works of capital
nature on the recommendations of Members of the Legislative Assembly (MLAs).
The performance audit of implementation of the Madhya Pradesh Assembly
Constituency Area Development Scheme revealed that the planning for
implementation of the scheme was not effective. Funds amounting to रैं 151.49 crore
remained unspent with the executing agencies. Unspent balances of f 96.29 lakh in
respect of 668 completed works could not be taken back from the executing agencies.
In 11 out of 12 test-checked districts, 717 works costing € 10.62 crore remained
incomplete. In six out of 12 test-checked districts, 111 works could not be started due
to selection of disputed sites. In seven out of 12 test-checked districts, completion
certificates in respect of 2,828 works were awaited from the executing agencies.
Quarterly meetings with MLAs and executing officers were not held regularly to
monitor the progress of works undertaken under the scheme. Due to non-
maintenance of a data base and asset registers, the public was largely unaware of the
work done under the scheme in their districts.

(Paragraph 1.1)
2 Construction of Bansagar Project Unit-II Canals

The construction of the Bansagar Project Unit-II canals was aimed at providing
irrigation in 2.49 lakh ha in the State through a 1463.70 km long canal network. The
project was taken up (1979) ata cost of € 10.06 crore for completion by 1987.

A performance audit of the construction of Bansagar Project-Unit Il-Canals revealed
improper planning, avoidable expenditure (€ 6.67 crore), undue benefit (® 7.23
crore), to a cement manufacturing company, awarding of supply order ( 57.89
crore) without inviting open tenders and blockage of Government money (® 4.02
crore). Mild steel pipes were procured at higher rates, resulting in extra cost of

ix


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

₹ 16.21 crore. Instead of constructing reinforced concrete cement footbridges, 1,648
mild steel footbridges were purchased ata higher rate, resulting in extra cost of 2.47
crore. Improper contract management led to excess payment of % 6.11 crore to
contractors. Performance security of € 2.10 crore and security deposit of € 1.10 crore
was released to contractors before completion of works. Even after spending
% 960.01 crore upto March 2010, only 911.51 km (against the envisaged 1463.70 km)
canal network was completed. Against the revised target of 1,23,634 ha, irrigation
potential of only 81,701 ha was developed. However, the actual irrigation from 2005
to 2010 remained as low as 4.19 to 15.67 per cent of the target.

(Paragraph 1.2)
3. Madhya Pradesh Water Sector Restructuring Project

The Madhya Pradesh Water Sector Restructuring Project (MPWSRP) was taken up
(May 2004) by the Water Resources Department (WRD), Government of Madhya
Pradesh. The project aimed at modernising the existing 654 irrigation schemes in
five selected river basins covering about 6.20 lakh ha spread area. WRD received a
loan of 1,919 crore from the World Bank for MPWSRP.

Performance Audit of MPWSRP revealed that the progress of implementation, both
physical and financial, was very slow. Out of the project cost of = 1,919 crore, only
% 688.25 crore (38 per cent) was utilised in five years (2005-10), out of the project
period of six years. Non-utilisation of funds led to avoidable liability of commitment
charges of 8.26 crore. Out of the 654 schemes earmarked in the project, only 100
schemes were taken up. Out of these, only nine schemes were completed in five
years. As against the targeted irrigation potential of 6.20 lakh ha, potential of 69,286
ha (i.e. 11.17 per cent) only was created. Delayed engagement of consultants, poor
financial management, deficient implementation of the IT system ete were
responsible for the slow implementation of the project. Irregular payment of € 26.65
crore to contractors due to non-conducting of mandatory tests of concrete; undue
favour to contractors by not obtaining additional security deposit of € 57.51 crore;
extra cost off one crore due to irregular award of work without invitation of tenders
and non-recovery of the liquidated damages of & 20.93 crore from contractors etc
were also noticed in audit.

(Paragraph 1.3)

4, Integrated Audit of Farmers Welfare and Agriculture Development
Department

The Farmers Welfare and Agriculture Development Department implements 14
State Plan and 19 Central sector/Centrally sponsored schemes aimed at increasing
the production and productivity of all types of crops. Integrated audit of the
department revealed inadequate financial controls, poor operational and quality
controls, deficient inventory control and lack of an internal control mechanism.



Overview

There were persistent savings ranging between 19 and 40 per cent during 2005-10.
Original budget provisions of € 40.78 crore were not utilised and supplementary
budget provisions of = 42.35 crore proved unnecessary. Funds amounting to
% 133.09 crore, pertaining to different schemes were lying in the bank accounts at
various levels, indicating deficiencies in cash management.

Targets set in the Tenth and Eleventh Five Year Plans for production of foodgrains
and oilseeds were not achieved. Seeds of older varities were distributed to the
farmers on which subsidy was also paid. Field officers did not ensure the distribution
of foundation/certified seeds and seed mini-kits before the cut-off dates. Adequate
attention was not paid to field demonstrations. Performance of Government
Agriculture Farms was poor and the available cultivable area was not fully utilised.
Expenditure of = 5.06 crore remained unfruitful on construction of Khet Taals and
Balram Taals as the farmers left the works incomplete and did not follow the
prescribed standards of construction.

Sub-standard seeds, fertilizers and pesticides were sold to cultivators having adverse
effect on production. Consumption of nitrogen, phosphate and potassium in the ratio
of 4:2:1 was not maintained in the fertilizers provided to cultivators. The
performance of quality testing laboratories, soil testing laboratories, soil surveys and
training was far below the set targets. There was no scientific assessment of
manpower requirement and deployment. Internal audit was inadequate and
ineffective. Monitoring was unsatisfactory as prescribed departmental inspections
were not conducted and district level committees were not functional.

(Paragraph 3.1)

5. Chief Controlling Officer based Audit of Animal Husbandry
Department

The Animal Husbandry Department is responsible for all-round development of
livestock wealth in the State. A Chief Controlling Officer based audit of the
department revealed that Plans were prepared on ad hoc basis without appropriate
database surveys, assessment of available resources and the actual requirements. The
tatio of the revenue and the capital expenditure during 2007-10 ranged between 26:1
and 725:1. Financial management lacked foresight leading to unnecessary
supplementary provisions, surrenders, parking of funds, diversion of funds and rush
of expenditure at the fag end of the financial year etc. The achievement of artificial
insemination fell short by 13 to 18 per cent and calving through artificial
insemination by eight to 23 per cent. The targets were reduced and heavy amounts
were surrendered or were lying unutilised in banks. Livestock distribution fell short
of targets. Veterinary farms were incurring losses as the production of eggs and wool
declined heavily. As against the requirement of 5,813 veterinary centres, only 2,346
centres were functioning. The hospitals were functioning without doctors. The feed
testing laboratory did not have alfa toxin, urea and mineral mixture estimation and
automatic analyzer facilities. The milk quality control laboratory and the bio-

xi


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

security laboratory level II were not established. Shortfall in artificial insemination
training to Veterinary Officers and Assistant Veterinary Field Officers ranged
between 45 and 72 per cent. Unserviceable goods worth हैं 1.30 crore were lying
undisposed of for periods upto 20 years. The Institute of Animal Husbandry and
Veterinary Biologicals was operating without a licence since 2002. Human resource
management was not satisfactory and there was a shortage of 17 to 41 per cent. The
Internal Audit Wing was not effective in the department and periodical inspections
were not being conducted by the departmental officers regularly.

(Paragraph 3.2)
6. Audit of Transactions

Audit of financial transactions in various departments of the Government and their
field functionaries revealed instances of losses, wasteful/excess expenditure,
avoidable expenditure and other irregularities involving € 34.34 crore.

Some of the important audit findings are given below:-

Failure to observe the codal provisions and Government orders facilitated
embezzlement/ misappropriation of € 1.35 crore in the office of the Commissioner,
Scheduled Caste Development, Bhopal.

(Paragraph 2.1.1)

Interpolations in the records coupled with lack of supervision led to suspected
embezzlement of % 12.32 lakh in the radio diagnosis department of Shyam Shah
Medical College, Rewa.

(Paragraph 2.1.2)

In the Pench Diversion Project, Water Resources Department, Chourai, a contractor
submitted a fake Fixed Deposit Receipt (FDR) of = 47 lakh toward earnest money
deposit for getting a contract valuing हैं 107 crore. Similarly, in the Pradhan Mantri
Gram Sadak Yojana works, release of security deposit against fake FDRs submitted
by a contractor in Ashoknagar district and failure to recover other dues of the
contractor resulted ina loss of 1.08 crore.

(Paragraph 2.1.8)

Due to execution of an agreement for higher contract demand in anticipation of
Government sanction for air-conditioning and delay in reduction in contract demand
apart from non-maintaining of the average monthly power factor, the State Museum
at Bhopal incurred avoidable expenditure off 54.59 lakh on electricity charges.

(Paragraph 2.3.1)

CHAPTER I
PERFORMANCE AUDIT

Planning, Economics and Statistics Department

1.1 Madhya Pradesh Assembly Constituency Area Development
Scheme

Highlights

The Government of Madhya Pradesh introduced the Madhya Pradesh Assembly

Constituency Area Development Scheme (MPACADS) in July 1994 under which

developmental works were to be taken up in each Assembly constituency area. A

performance audit of the scheme covering the period 2005-10 revealed that alarge
number of works were lying incomplete; asset registers were not maintained;
details of completed works transferred to concerned departments/agencies were
not available; inspections of works were not carried out regularly and monitoring
was inadequate. Monthly income-expenditure statements and progress reports of
works were not submitting regularly by the executing agencies.

Some important findings of the performance review are given below:-

The District Planning Officers (DPOs) did not release = 18.60 crore to the
implementing agencies. There were delays in release of funds to the executing
agencies ranging from 37 to 980 days.

(Paragraphs 1.1.7.2 and 1.1.7.3)

The DPO Bhind irregularly parked Z 1.93 crore in a Personal Deposit Account.
A total unspent amount of = 96.29 lakh in respect of 668 completed works was
lying with the executing agencies.

(Paragraphs 1.1.7.4 and 1.1.7.5)

Out of 68,728 sanctioned works, 55,953 works were completed while 10,522
works remained incomplete, 2,067 works were not taken up and 186 works were
cancelled.

(Paragraph 1.1.8.1)

In seven districts, 814 works costing हैं 13.91 crore were sanctioned with delays
ranging from 25 to 431 days.

(Paragraph 1.1.8.2)

In six districts, 111 works costing = one crore could not be started due to
selection of disputed sites and the funds were lying with the executing agencies.
Completion certificates in respect of 2,828 works costing = 40.63 crore, were
awaited from the agencies.

(Paragraphs 1.1.8.4 and 1.1.8.5)


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Records pertaining to transfer of assets to concerned departments/ agencies for
use were either incomplete or were not made available. The Commissioner did
not maintain complete/updated information of works selected under the
scheme. Registers of assets were not found maintained in the test-checked
districts.

(Paragraph 1.1.9.2)

The nodal agencies failed to monitor the implementation of the scheme by way
of inspection of works.

(Paragraph 1.1.10.1)

1.1.1 Introduction

The Madhya Pradesh Assembly Constituency Area Development Scheme
(MPACADS) was launched in July 1994, covering all the 231 Assembly
constituencies with the objective of taking up developmental works of capital nature
on the recommendations of the Members of Legislative Assembly (MLAs) for
creation of community assets based on local needs in their respective constituencies.

According to the scheme guidelines, works like construction of rural fair-weather
roads, approach roads, internal bituminous roads, footpaths, footbridges, kharanjas
in villages, chabutaras, buildings for government aided/unaided educational
institutions, bus stops/sheds, public reading rooms, small irrigation facilities,
drinking water facilities, crematoria, tubewells, gardens, sports complexes as well as
works connected with social forestry, agricultural development, culture ete were
required to be taken up under the scheme. The works were to be meant for public
purposes and were to be completed in one or at the most two seasons. Purchase of
inventory or equipment, repairs and maintenance of existing works, construction of
memorials, works of religious nature and acquisition of land or any compensation for
land acquired were specifically disallowed under the scheme.

1.1.2 Organisational Set-up

At the State level, the Commissioner, Economic and Statistics (Commissioner) is
responsible for implementation and monitoring of the scheme. The District Planning
Officers (DPOs) are the nodal officers of the schemes at the district level. The works
under the scheme are executed by various implementing agencies such as the
Education, Farmers Welfare and Agriculture Development, Housing and
Environment, Public Health & Family Welfare, Public Works, Public Health
Engineering and Rural Panchayat and Development departments.

1.1.3 Audit Objectives

The objectives of the performance audit were to assess whether:

> the planning process was adequate;



Chapter-I Performance Audit

> the funds were being managed economically, effectively and efficiently;

v

the works were being sanctioned and executed as per the scheme
guidelinesand;

v

the monitoring mechanism was effective.

1.1.4 Audit Criteria

The main criteria used for the performance audit were:

> Scheme guidelines and instructions issued by the State Government/
Commissioner;

> Madhya Pradesh Treasury Code, Madhya Pradesh Financial Code and
Madhya Pradesh Store-Purchase Rules; and

> Provisions of agreements governing execution and maintenance of assets.

1.1.5 Scope and Methodology of Audit

The performance audit of the records of the Commissioner, 12 DPO's
(Appendix-1.1) selected on the basis of Probability Proportionate to Size Sampling
method and 94 executing agencies selected randomly was conducted during April to
December 2010 for the period 2005-10. An entry conference was held with the
Commissioner on 26 March 2010, wherein the audit objectives of performance audit,
criteria, scope and methodology were explained. An exit conference was held with
the Principal Secretary, Government of Madhya Pradesh, Planning, Economics and
Statistics Department on 4 December 2010. Government's views have been
incorporated in the review. Results of test check are included in the succeeding
paragraphs.

Audit Findings

1.1.6 Planning

As per Para 3 of the guidelines, the District Collectors (DC) were required to obtain
written proposals of works proposed by MLAs or hold meetings in the first quarter of
the financial year to identify the works to be taken up and listed together with
Centrally Sponsored and Central Sector programmes. The works included in the list
were required to be entrusted to executing agencies for execution. Lists of accepted
works for execution and rejected proposals were to be sent to the MLAs within 45
days of the meetings. However, it was noticed that during 2005-10, no such meeting
was arranged in any of the test-checked districts and works were undertaken on the
basis of recommendations of the MLAs.

1.1.7 Financial Management

1.1.7.1 Funding pattern

The MPACADS is a State funded scheme. The funds for implementation of the
scheme are placed at the disposal of the DPOs by the department through the budget.
Funds of € 60 lakh during 2005-06, = 80 lakh during 2006-07 and thereafter 77
lakh, was allocated per year to each MLA for recommending works of capital nature
to be executed in his/her Assembly constituency. Funds were released by the DPOs
directly to the implementing agencies in instalments. In cases where Gram
Panchayats are the implementing agencies, the funds were released through Janpad
Panchayats.

1.1.7.2 Allocation of funds and expenditure

Out of हैं 857.01 crore allocated by the Government during 2005-10 for
implementation of the scheme, % 838.41 crore was released by the DPOs to the
agencies for execution of the works, against which = 686.92 crore were reportedly
spent by the agencies as indicated in Table-1.1.

1.1.7.3 Delay in release of funds

According to instructions (January 2007) of the Commissioner, funds were to be
provided to executing agencies within 15 days after the sanction of works.

Scrutiny of records of Janpad Panchayats of seven districts’ revealed that the funds
were released to Gram Panchayats with delays ranging from 37 to 980 days as shown
in Table-1.2 below.

Panchayats stated (May to December 2010) that due to non-completion of the
formalities by the executing agencies such as submission of agreements, land details,
map and technical sanctions of the works, the amounts could not be released in time.
During the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that the CEOs had been
instructed to adhere to the guidelines for timely release of funds in future.

1.1.8.2 Delay in sanctioning the works

According to the instructions (January 2007) of the Commissioner, administrative
sanctions of works recommended by MLAs under the scheme were to be issued
within 30 days of the receipt of the proposals.

Out of 12 test-checked DPOs, scrutiny of the records in seven districts’ revealed that

1.1.8.6 Irregular payment of supervision charges

According to instructions issued (August 1999) by the Commissioner, no
supervision/contingent charges were chargeable on the works executed by the
agencies under the scheme.

Scrutiny of records of three districts revealed that supervision/contingent charges
amounting to 6.56 lakh for 41" works were paid to agencies (PWD, MPSEB and
Janpad Panchayats) in contravention of standing instructions.

On this being pointed out, the DPOs stated (March to December 2010) that the cases
would be reviewed and appropriate action for the recovery would be taken. During
the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that instructions had been issued to
recover the amount paid as supervision charges from executing agencies.

1.1.9.1 Non-maintenance of the information of works

Para 4.3 of the scheme guidelines stipulates that the Commissioner should have up to
date information of works completed under the scheme. Further, according to the
instructions issued (April 1996) by the Commissioner, a complete data base was
tequired to be prepared in respect of completed works under the schemes in National
Informatics Centre (NIC) at the district level.

Scrutiny of records of the Commissioner, for the year 2005-10, revealed non-

On this being pointed out, the Commissioner stated (March 2010) that it was not
possible to have consolidated information for all constituencies at State level. The
teply isnot acceptable as it was contrary to the guidelines.

The preparation of a data base is a vital requirement in respect of the completed
works in districts to apprise the public of the works done in their districts. Due to non-
maintenance of the data base, the public was largely unaware of works done under
the scheme in their districts.

At the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that the DPOs had been
instructed to maintain a database at the district level.

1.1.9.2 Assets created under the scheme were neither recorded nor verifiable

As per the circular issued (July 2003) by the Commissioner, the assets created under
the scheme, were required to be recorded in a separate asset register and the assets
were to be handed over to the concerned department/agency foruse.

During test check of records in the 12 selected districts”, it was noticed that the
records of assets transferred were not available with the DPOs. Similarly registers of
assets were not maintained by the DPOs in nine districts" and were incomplete in
three districts".

The Principal Secretary during exit conference stated that the DPOs had been
instructed to maintain asset registers and transfer the created assets.

1.1.10 Monitoring

1.1.10.1 Lack ofmonitoring

Para 4 of the scheme guidelines provide that the DPO is responsible for overall co-
ordination and supervision of the works at the district level and is required to inspect
at least 10 per cent of the works executed within his jurisdiction under the scheme.

On this being pointed out, the DPOs stated (June to December 2010) that monitoring
could not be done as desired in the guidelines due to inadequate staff. At the exit
conference, the Principal Secretary stated that efforts were being made to fill up
vacant posts and the DPOs had been instructed to ensure proper monitoring in future.

1.1.10.2 Non-holding quarterly meeting at District level

As per the instructions issued (April 1996) by the Commissioner, it was obligatory
for the District Collector to hold quarterly meetings with MLAs and executing
officers to apprise the MLAs, of progress of the works taken up under the scheme.

During the scrutiny of the records of 12 districts”, it was revealed that no such
meetings were held during 2005-10.

On being pointed out, the DPOs (June to December 2010) assured that they would
hold the quarterly meeting regularly in future. At the exit conference, the Principal
Secretary stated that instructions had been reiterated to conduct regular meetings.

1.1.10.3 Progress reports submitted by DPOs without obtaining expenditure
statements from agencies

As per the instructions (April 1996) of the Commissioner, the monthly income-
expenditure statements and progress reports were required to be obtained by the
DPOs from the executing agencies for the funds released to them for onward
submission to the Commissioner.

Scrutiny of records of 12 selected districts” revealed that none of the 94 executing
agencies submitted regularly monthly income-expenditure statements and the
progress reports to the DPOs.

On this being pointed out, the Principal Secretary stated that the DPOs had been
instructed to obtain monthly income and expenditure statements from the executing
agencies in future.

Chapter-I Performance Audit

1.1.11 Conclusion

The works were undertaken without any planning for implementation of the scheme.
Funds available under the scheme were not fully utilised. There were cases of
short/delayed release of funds, parking of funds in personal deposit account and non-
recovery of balances from executing agencies. There were delays in sanctioning and
completion of works. Completion certificates were wanting in about 43 per cent
works, Asset registers were not maintained. There was lack of monitoring through
inspection of works.

1.1.12 Recommendations

> Periodic evaluations should be conducted to assess the actual utility and
public benefits being derived out of the works sanctioned so as to facilitate
MLAs in selecting works as per the felt needs of their constituencies.

Vv

Asset registers should be maintained so as to enable the verification of assets
created under the scheme. Suitable arrangements should be devised to ensure
the transfer of assets to user departments and maintenance of the assets
created.

> Asystem of online tracking and monitoring of execution of works should be
devised for timely action against delays in execution and completion of
works.

Water Resources Department

1.2 Construction of Bansagar Project Unit-II Canals

Highlights
Construction of Unit-I head work of Bansagar project, taken up (1978) at a cost of
% 91.31 crore, was completed (2006) at a cost of %1,251 crore. Construction of a 1463.27
km network and 3,351 structures of Bansagar Project Unit-II Canal were taken up (1979)
at a cost of 210.06 crore for completion by 1987. After spending %961.01 crore upto
March 2010, only 911.51 km canal network and 1,857 structures were completed. Against
a target of 1,23,634 hectares, irrigation potential of only 81,701 hectares was developed.
The actual irrigation potential utilised was as low as 4.19 per cent to 15.67 per cent of the
proposed irrigation potential.
Some important findings of performance audit of Unit-II canal works are given below:
The estimates and designs of excavation and structures were prepared without proper
survey. Variations in quantities led to an extra expenditure of = 13.32 crore. Funds
amounting to ¥ 43.82 crore provided for construction of the Bansagar canal network
were diverted and utilised for construction of Lift Irrigation Schemes (LISs) and
Magardaha canal of Budwa minor tank.

(Paragraphs 1.2.6.2 and 1.2.7.1)

Construction of the Mahidalkalan distributory and the Naybasta distributory was
badly delayed. Absence of co-ordination with the Uttar Pradesh Irrigation Department
led to an avoidable expenditure of € 6.67 crore and blockage of Government money of
% 4,02 crore. A cement manufacturing company was given an undue advantage of € 7.23
crore.

(Paragraphs 1.2.8.3, 1.2.8.4 and 1.2. 8.5)

Mild steel (MS) pipe aqueducts constructed at a cost of 5.67 crore were not capable of
passing the designed discharge. Earthwork costing हैं 53.17 lakh and a cement concrete
toe wall worth 3.17 crore were below specifications.
(Paragraphs 1.2.8.6, 1.2.8.7 and 1.2. 8.8)
Purchase of MS footbridges and MS signboards through an unauthorised agency led to
an extra cost of % 2.47 crore and MS footbridges worth हैं 19.46 crore were purchased
without proper design. There was an excess purchase of 1,317 footbridges, out of which
593 footbridges were not installed.
(Paragraphs 1.2.8.10 and 1.2. 8.11)
Contrary to the codal requirements, the work of design, supply, fabrication and
erection of VT pumps, LISs worth हैं 50.15 crore and MS pipe rising main and
aqueducts of € 57.69 crore was entrusted to suppliers through Madhya Pradesh Laghu
Udyog Nigam without inviting open tenders. Purchase of MS pipes at a higher rate
resulted in extra cost of 16.21 crore.
(Paragraph 1.2.8.9)

2.1 Introduction

The Bansagar multipurpose project on the river Sone is an inter-State project of
Bihar, Madhya Pradesh (MP) and Uttar Pradesh (UP). The project envisaged
creation of an irrigation potential of 2.49 lakh hectares (ha) with 125 per cent
irrigation intensity in MP, 1.50 lakh ha in UP and stabilizing the irrigation
requirements of 94000 ha of the old Sone canal system of Bihar. The project also
envisaged hydro-power generation of 425 megawatt for MP. Unit-I of the project
comprising construction of the headwork was started in 1978 at a cost of 91.31
crore and was completed in September 2006 at a cost of
% 1,251 crore. Construction of the canal system under Unit-II of the project in each
State was the responsibility of that State.

Construction of Bansagar Project Unit-II
Canals in MP was taken up in 1979 at a
cost of = 10.06 crore and was still in
progress as of March 2010, after spending
= 961.01 crore. The canal system
comprised seven canals including two lift
irrigation schemes (LIS). It covered an
irrigable command area (ICA) of 1,99,487
ha. The Unit-II canals included construction of 445.52 km long main canal, the
distributaries and the minors of each canal. The canal network at a glance is given in
Table 1.11.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

1.2.2 Organisational Setup

The Water Resources Department (WRD) of the State is headed by the Principal
Secretary. The Engineer-in-Chief (E-in-C) is the apex level field officer. For the
Bansagar project, he is assisted by one Chief Engineer (CE), Ganga Basin, Rewa and
two Superintending Engineers (SEs), Bansagar Canal Circle and Masonry Dam
Circle, who are responsible for overall planning, implementation and supervision of
works, At the field level, six Executive Engineers (EEs) are responsible for the
execution of works under the direction and control of the CE and the SEs. The
organogram of the department is given in Appendix 1.3.

1.2.3 Audit objectives

The performance audit was conducted to assess whether:
> the project was planned appropriately;

> the financial management and the utilisation of funds were proper and
effective;

the execution of the project was carried out economically and efficiently;

v

> the project works were executed as per plan, standard design and
specifications and were completed within the stipulated time frame and

> the monitoring system was adequate and effective.

1.2.4 Auditcriteria

The audit findings were based on the following criteria:

> Provisions of the Works Department Manual and orders/ instructions issued
by the Government and the Central Water Commission.

Approved designs and irrigation specifications issued by the Government for
construction of canals.

v

> Standard codes of Bureau of Indian Standards.

1.2.5 Scope ofaudit and methodology

The scope of the performance audit was restricted to the construction of
Unit-II canals of the Bansagar project. The period of audit coverage was from 2005-
06 to 2009-10. Records of the offices of the E-in-C, CE, SE and all the divisions were
reviewed between July and October 2010 to the extent the records made available by
the department. Some records like supply orders and vouchers for purchases,
measurement books, cash books etc for the period 2005-06 to 2007-08 had been
seized by the Economic Offences Wing (EOW) and the Lokayukt, Government of
Madhya Pradesh.

An entry conference was held (April 2010) with the Principal Secretary and other
officials of the WRD and the exit conference was held (December 2010) with the

16



Chapter-I Performance Audit

Additional Secretary and other officials of the WRD. The views of the department
have been incorporated in the review at appropriate places.

Audit acknowledges the co-operation given by WRD during the course of
performance audit.

1.2.6 Audit findings
1.2.6.1 Planning

The scope of the Bansagar canal (Unit-I]) was limited to providing water to the
agricultural fields (2.49 lakh ha) through main canals, distributory canals and minor
canals. The project approved (1978) at 10.06 crore by the Government was to be
completed by June 1987. However, it got clearance from the Planning Commission
in November 2001 for ₹ 344.66 crore at the 1991 price level. Revision in cost from
time to time and shifting of target dates of completion are shown in graph below:

1.2.6.2 Deficiency in the planning process

According to paragraphs 2.025 and 2.017 of the Works Department (WD) Manual,
estimates for works were required to be prepared on the basis of detailed surveys and
trial pits to determine, as accurately as possible, the quantities of various items of
work to be executed. Para 2.028 of the WD Manual further provided that the officer
according the technical sanction to an estimate was responsible for ensuring the
soundness of design, by incorporating all the items.

During the course of audit, instances of defective estimates, estimation of
disproportionate inputs, execution of works beyond the scope of Administrative
Approval (AA) etc were noticed which are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs:

>

In the work of construction of packages KCP-3, PCP-6, PCP-10 and PCP 11-
A, the estimates for excavation in canals were prepared without proper
survey and as a result, the quantities of various types of strata vis-a-vis the
estimated quantities abnormally varied as detailed in Appendix 1.4.
Consequently, the department had to incur (August 2007 to June 2010) extra
expenditure of & 6.77 crore in four cases for execution of the excess
quantities.

Construction of the distributaries and the minors between km 32.95 to km
41.41 and 238 structures” (package KCP-9) of Keoti Main Canal was taken
up (December 2006) at a cost of = 6.92 crore. The detailed estimates were
prepared on the basis of sample design of the structures. Due to defective
estimation of cement concrete likely to be consumed in the works, the entire
quantity of 22318.48 cu m cement concrete (CC) 1:3:6 was consumed
(March 2009) for the construction of 84 structures only. To complete the
work, the additional quantities of 34,277.603 cu m CC involving % 6.55 crore
were re-estimated. The revised quantities, however, had not been approved
by the Governmentas of July 2010.

The EE stated (August 2010), that the matter would be investigated and
reported to Audit. The reply was awaited as of December 2010.

Budwa tank, an old minor irrigation tank (outside the scope of Bansagar
Project) in Shahdol district was designed for irrigable command area (ICA)
of 800 ha. In April 2005, the department noticed that the Right Bank Canal
(RBC) system of the Bansagar canals was overlapping” the ICA of 600 ha of
the Budwa tank. In order to rectify it, the E-in-C, WRD transferred (April
2005) the Budwa tank to the Bansagar project. The CE, Bansagar provided
(2009) ₹ 10.50 crore in the revised administrative approval of the Bansagar
canal for construction of the eight km Magardaha canal at Budwa tank to
restore ICA of 600 ha and expenditure of € 5.09 crore was incurred up to 2009
for the construction of Magardaha canal. This resulted in diversion of = 5.09
crore in the construction of Magardaha canal from the funds earmarked for
the Bansagar canal.

The EE, Rockfill Dam Division, Deolond stated (September 2010) that the
decision for construction of the Magardaha canal system of Budwa tank was
taken by the higher authorities.

1.2.8 Project execution

For efficient and cost-effective execution of works relating to the project, specific
targets and milestones were prescribed for execution and creation of the specified
irrigation potential to avoid delay and ensure timely accrual of the intended benefits.
The works were to be executed as per the provisions of the Madhya Pradesh Works
Department Manual and the specifications issued by the E-in-C.

Instances of deficiencies and departure from the provision of the Manual and
specifications are discussed below:

1.2.8.1 Non-achievement of targets

In order to complete the works of the main canals and the distribution system
excluding the Gurh-Mauganj and the Teonthar LIS by June 2009, the Unit II canal
project was divided into three phases (Phase —I by June 2005, Phase I-A by June
2008 and Phase II-B by June 2009). While including the project for the NABARD
loan, the date of completion was revised from June 2009 to March 2010. The
lengthwise target of each phase was recorded by the department every year.

Scrutiny in audit revealed that the progress of the three canal systems, namely
Sihawal, Keoti and Purwa canals ranged between 44 to 80 per cent and the
construction of structures remained as 55.42 per cent as on 31 March 2010. The slow
progress was mainly due to the following reasons:

> In 20 out of 44 cases test checked, the works were awarded as late as 2008-09.

> There were delays in completion of works by the contractors from one year to
four and half years with respect to the stipulated dates of completion in 25 out
of 44 cases test-checked.

> The works of Purwa and Keoti main canals were taken up in the years 2001
and 2003 respectively. The distributory division assigned with the works of
construction of distributories for these canals was established in 2003 but it
started the work in 2006.

The CE stated (September 2010) that yearwise targets and achievements were
maintained with respect to the quantities of works and notas per the length of canal.
He further stated that the work would be completed by June 2012. The reply is not
acceptable as the quantities of earthwork already exceeded the estimated quantities.
As against the estimated quantities of excavation of 31787.601 thousand cu m,
31966.81 thousand cu m excavation was already executed up to March 2010, while
the length of the canal completed upto March 2010 ranged from 44.06 per cent
(Purwa canal) to 80.16 per cent (Sihawal canal). The progress with respect to
executed quantities, therefore, did not convey the actual status of completion.

After the entry conference held in April 2010 with the Principal Secretary, WRD, the
Government constituted (July 2010) a Review Committee headed by the Additional
Secretary, WRD to review the physical status of the project. A gist of some of the
important observations of the Review Committee (submitted to the Government in
September 2010) relating to sub-standard execution of canal works and works not
conforming to the specification and designs are givenin Appendix 1.5.

1.2.8.3 Delay due to change of alignment

Construction of the Mahidalkalan distributory and the Navbasta distributory was
awarded (June 2005 and December 2006) to two different contractors for
% 6.76 crore and & 8.92 crore respectively. The contractors were paid (June 2010 and
September 2009) ₹ 2.95 crore and ₹ 2.07 crore respectively. An amount of & 1.36
crore and ₹ 2.76 crore was incurred on land acquisition. During construction, it was
noticed that a portion of four km (from km 2.40 to km 6.40) of the Mahidalkalan
distributory and the Navbasta distributory was aligned on leased land of a lime stone
mine of a cement manufacturing company. On the request (March 2007) of the
company, the Government decided (August 2008) to divert the alignment of the
canals with the condition that the company would bear the total expenditure of the
construction on the new alignment as well as the expenditure already incurred by the

22. Proposedirrigation 123634 ha at 125 per cent irrigation intensity = 154543 ha.

Chapter-I Performance Audit

department on land acquisition and the construction of the canals on the old
alignment.

Scrutiny in auditrevealed that though a period of two years had passed, an amount of
% 2.40 crore” and® 4.83 crore” incurred on construction and land acquisition had not
been demanded from the company and the new alignment of canal was still to be
surveyed. Thus not only was the construction of the canal delayed but undue
advantage of 7.23 crore was also extended to the company.

The EEs stated (July and September 2010) that the company had deposited
(September 2010) हैं 8.17 lakh and हैं 6.58 lakh for the survey work. The survey of
Mahidalkalan distributory was pending due to objections by the cultivators and the
progress of survey on the new alignment of Navabasta distributory would be
intimated. The reply is not acceptable because the expenditure already incurred had
not been demanded from the company.

1.2.8.4 Avoidable expenditure due to absence of co-ordination

Absence of According to technical circular 70/1 issued by the E-in-C, WRD (June 1980),
co-ordination led whenever a contour canal crosses a number of drainages along its course, appropriate
expenditure of drainage crossings have to be provided. It is possible in a few cases, to combine two
% 6.67 crore. or more drainages and construct only one crossing. Judicious discretion, based on

preliminary technical examination, should be used in deciding whether or not a

drainage is to be diverted to the adjoining one.

The Sihawal Main Canal of MP and UP Feeder Canal (UPFC) of UP are contour
canals. The UPFC offtakes at km 15.24 of the Sihawal Main Canal and thereafter
runs parallel to the Sihawal canal at a distance of 150 m to 200 m at ahigher altitude
in the Kaimore hills. Construction of the Sihawal canal was partially completed by
February 1998 while the work of UPFC was started in 1998. As per the Detailed
Project Report, the Government had planned 108 structures like drainage siphon and.
drainage culverts between km 15.25 to km 75.27 at a cost of % 14.05 crore, out of
which & 12.94 crore had already been incurred upto March 2009. The UP Irrigation
Department (UPID), constructed only 16 big structures on UPFC but not in the front
of the structures of the Sihawal canal. The small structures constructed on the
Sihawal canal were not sufficient to pass the heavy discharge of water coming from
the big structures of UPFC, without damaging the Sihawal canal. According to the
CE Ganga basin, Rewa, (August 2006) the situation required construction of 14 new
structures and renovation of four existing structures at an estimated cost of = 5.50
crore. Had the work been planned properly in coordination with UPID keeping in

plan of UPFC and the department failed to coordinate with UPID.

1.2.8.5 Blockage of funds due to non-construction of head regulator of a
distributory

The Detailed Project Report of 1999 provided for construction of the 4.68 km long
Shikarganj distributory at km 0.21 of the Sihawal Main Canal for creating an
irrigable command area (ICA) of 2762 ha. The work was awarded (March 2001) toa
contractor. At the time of the layout, it was noticed by the department that the ground
level in the approved estimates differed from the initial ground levels. Therefore, the
efficiency and the utility of the distributory became disputable. It was, therefore,
decided (April 2002) to construct another 13.30 km long distributory No. 2 from km
10.80 of common water carrier (CWC) for ICA of 1454 ha by reducing ICA of the
earlier distributory to 1170 ha. Pending a decision on the construction of the head
tegulator of distributory No. 2, the work was awarded (December, 2002) to a
contractor who had been paid ₹ 4.02 crore (August, 2008). Permission to construct a
head regulator at km 10.80 of the CWC was requested (January 2005) from the
MPSEB since the CWC was under their control. Being an inter-State matter, MPSEB
required (February 2005) permission from the Bansagar Control Board. No action
had been initiated by the CE, Ganga basin from the Control Board as of September
2010 to seek the requisite permission. Thus the expenditure (% 4.02 crore) incurred on
construction of the Shikarganj distributory No.2, without constructing the head
regulator resulted in blockage of Government money.

The EE stated (September 2010) that the construction of Shikarganj distributory
No.2 was in progress and water at the head of the canal would be provided by the
siphon system. The reply is not acceptable because there was no correspondence
with the MPSEB regarding construction of the siphon except the head regulator and
permission for the siphon at CWC had not been obtained from MPSEB and the
Control Board.

1.2.8.6 Purchase of inappropriate Mild Steel pipes

(i) 176 CE, Ganga Basin, Rewa approved (July 2007) an estimate for
construction of an MS pipe aqueduct at km 60.06 of the Keoti Main Canal for
% 2.32 crore. The estimate provided use of MS pipes of 1,800 mm dia in the
thickness of 10 mm in two rows. The canal was having discharge of 5.122
cumecs and bed slope of 1 in 6,000 but while calculating the discharge, the
department adopted (July 2007) a steeper slope of 1 in 1500 to match the

design discharge from the two pipes. Payment of = 96.09 lakh was made

Scrutiny in audit revealed that on the basis of the bed slope of the canal 1 in

passing discharge of 2.75 cumecs only. Thus the purchase of pipe 1800 mm
dia atthe cost of = 96.09 lakh was unfruitful.

(ii) The CE approved (January and July 2007) construction of MS pipe
aqueducts of 2,000 mm dia, 10 mm thick in two rows, at km 112.50 and km
113.16 km of Purwa Main Canal having discharge of 5.309 cumecs at bed
slope of 1 in 5,500. However, the department prepared the design by adopting
the steeper slope of 1 in 1,500 to match the design discharge.

Scrutiny in audit revealed that on the basis of bed slope of the canal 1 in
5,500, the MS pipe aqueduct in two rows of 2,000 mm dia was capable of
passing discharge of 3.812 cumecs only. Thus the amount of & 4.71 crore
incurred (March 2007 to March 2010) on procurement of MS pipes was
unfruitful. Further, the Chief Technical Examiner (CTE) observed (April
2010) that the MS pipes supplied for aqueduct at km 113.16 got deformed
and were getting rusted because the pipes were not coated with quality paints.

The EE stated (July 2010) that the canal was in the tail area where the supply
of water was automatically reduced and the canal bed level (CBL) so reduced
would be maintained because reduction in CBL would range upto 30 cm
only. The reply is not relevant because the section of canal was approved for
discharge of 5.309 cumecs and reduction in the canal bed level would
ultimately reduce the Irrigable Command Area (ICA).

1.2.8.7 Non-execution of works as per specification

According to the irrigation specifications forming part of the agreement, earthwork
for canal embankment is required to be executed layer by layer in 15 cm thickness,
compacted at optimum moisture content to the maximum dry density. As per the
Unified schedule of rates (USR), a deduction of 10 per cent should be made from the
compacted quantity of earthwork on account of shrinkage allowance before making
payment for compacted earthwork. Further, the USR also provides that if the item of
earthwork is neither rolled nor watered, a deduction of 20 per cent is to be made from
the executed quantity of earthwork

As per the item of earthwork mentioned in the agreement for the construction of the
package PCP-13A-I, the quantity of earthwork 386704.61 cu m executed and paid
(June 2010) was required to be compacted to 90 per cent. Scrutiny in audit revealed
that the quantity of 106339.64 cum earthwork was paid after deduction of 20 per cent
shrinkage allowance instead of 10 per cent. Deduction of 20 per cent shrinkage
allowance indicated that the earthwork was done without rolling and watering,
which was an essential part of the complete earthwork. Thus the earthwork done
without watering and compaction amounting 0 = 53.17 lakh at 50 per cu m was
substandard.

25



Execution of
substandard
cement concrete
toe wall
amounting to

₹ 3.17 crore.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

The EE, Distibutory Division, Rewa stated (August 2010) that deduction of 20 per
cent was made from the executed quantity to restrict the quantities. The reply is not
acceptable because as per the Unified Schedule of Rates, the deduction of 20 per cent
from contractor's payment was to be made from earthwork ‘neither rolled nor
watered’. Thus the deduction of 20 per cent from the executed quantity signified that
the earthwork was neither rolled nor watered.

1.2.8.8 Construction of sub-standard cement concrete toe wall

The UPFC offtakes from km 15.24 in the left side of the Sihawal canal of MP and
aligns parallel at a distance of 150 m to 200 m from each other ata higher level in the
Kaimore hills. The works of UPFC started in 1998 and were in progress. The
excavation of the Sihawal Canal was taken up in 2002. During construction, the
executing agency of UPFC placed the excavated material at its right bank which fell
into the Sihawal Canal and damaged the canal. Asa protection, the UPID constructed.
a stone masonry toe wall at km 7.60 to km 8.60 of the UPFC (km 22 and km 23 of the
Sihawal canal).

As the one km long toe wall
constructed by UPID was not
sufficiently protecting the
Sihawal Canal of MP (being
# constructed alongside at a lower
level), the CE, Bansagar
approved (September 2006) an
estimate of = 19.58 crore for the
construction of a 14.1 km long
and one to five metre high cement concrete (CC) reinforced toe wall (between km
15.24 and km 75.27) at the left bank of the Sihawal Canal to protect it from the
slippages of UPFC. The work was awarded (2007-08) to a contractor for = 18.20
crore who completed (June 2010) the work for 8.50 km only and was paid (June
2010) an amount of 15.04 crore.

View of stone masonry toe wall of UPFC.

Audit scrutiny (July 2010) of the records revealed that for construction of the CC toe
wall at km 15.24 to km 75.27 of the Sihawal Main Canal, the contractor was to
provide CC 1:2:4 by using 40 mm metal (40 per cent), 20 mm metal (35 per cent) and
10 mm metal (25 per cent). The required compressive strength of the concrete at 28
days was 150 kg per sq cm. Audit scrutiny revealed that as per results of the quality
control tests conducted by the Assistant Research Officer, Quality Control unit at km
42.8 to km 43.3, the contractor had used oversized metal up to 80 mm in concrete.
The compressive strength of the concrete (at km 20.285 to km 21.5, km 25 and km
42.8 to 43.3) was as low as 107 kg per sq cm to 145 kg per sq cm. The metal used in
concrete was absorbing water more than the prescribed limit of one per cent. The EE
also had observed (April to September 2009) that the metal contained dust. The work
of%3.17 crore executed on above location was therefore, substandard.

1.2.8.9 Award of a composite work at higher rate without inviting tenders

Paragraph 2.077 of the WD Manual required that for each work amounting to
% two lakh and above, the department should invite tenders in a transparent manner
through publicity in newspapers to get reasonable rates. As per the MP Stores
Purchase Rules, the Madhya Pradesh Laghu Udyog Nigam (MPLUN), was an
indenting agency, only for supply of the reserved items of stores specified in the MP
Stores Purchase Rules. However, in case the department required an item of a
particular specification suiting to their requirements, the MPLUN, was to invite
tenders accordingly. The respective purchasing department was to nominate their
representatives in MPLUN for opening the tenders and finalisation of the rates.
Construction works and erection of new mechanical works did not come under the
purview of purchases.

Contrary to these codal requirements, the contract for works of design, supply,
installation, erection and commissioning of VT pumps and rising main of MS pipes
for Gurh-Mauganj LIS and Teonthar LIS and MS pipe aqueduct at various canals was
given to the suppliers through MPLUN at higher rates without inviting open tenders
in the following cases:

> The CE, Ganga basin, Rewa approved (November 2007) an estimate at
% 6.25 crore for supply of VT pumps and motors, including all accessories for
Gurh-Mauganj LIS including installation work. Lump sum tenders for
planning, design, engineering, manufacturing, supply etc. for VT pumps and
motors, including all accessories, were invited (February 2008) and
submitted to the Government. The Principal Secretary, WRD directed
(September 2008) the CE, to get the work done through some other method
instead of lump sum tender and returned the tenders (price bid) unopened. At
the instructions of CE, the SE placed (January 2009) an indent to MPLUN to
get the work done at their rate contract. MPLUN issued (May 2009) a direct
supply order for हैं 26.22 crore for one job of ‘design, supply instalation,
erection, commissioning, operation and maintenance, design of VT pumps,
motors and associated electrical equipments' to a firm of Indore on lump sum
basis. Thus the work was awarded at higher rate of = 19.96 crore in
comparison with the estimated rates without inviting tenders by the
department and without any justification of rates prevailing as on date or the
tates available in the rate list of the original manufacturer.

> The item of VT pumps was not a reserve item” for purchase through the
MPLUN. The SE directed (June, 2009) the EE to execute the supplementary

Also, the purchases were made without inviting tenders.

> The CE permitted (January and June 2009) purchase of ‘high intensity

₹ 12,923 per sqm. After deducting 13.87 per cent of the prevailing market
tates as per the accepted tender, the rate of one signboard worked out to
₹ 33,392. Thus the purchase was made at higher rates, involving extra cost of
₹ 1.22 crore.

The EE, Bansagar Distributory Division, Rewa stated (August 2010) that the
purchase was sanctioned by the SE and the payment was made accordingly.

The reply is not acceptable because MPCCF is not the authorised agency for
purchase of footbridges. Also, the purchases were made without inviting tenders.

1.2.9 Contract management

For construction and other works awarded to contractors, execution and payments
were to be governed by the provisions in the agreements, provisions of the MP Works
Department Manual as well as related instructions issued from time to time by the
higher authorities. Security deposit and performance security deposit were required
tobe obtained from the contractors inthe manner prescribed in the agreement or rules.

Instances of violation of agreement provisions and provisions of the Manual leading
to inadmissible payments are discussed in the succeeding paragraph.

1.2.9.1 Undue benefit to the contractor

> According to clauses 2.8, 4.2.1 and 4.3 of the agreement, the contractor
whose tender was to be accepted was required, within 30 days of receiving
the letter of acceptance or before entering the agreement, to furnish an initial
performance security deposit equal to five per cent of the contract value. In
addition, deduction for security deposit was to be made from the running bills
equal to five per cent of each bill. However, the contractor was authorised to
get the security deposit converted into interest-bearing securities. The
validity of the performance security was up to 30 months beyond the due date
of completion of works. The security deposit was to be refunded after the
expiry of 12 months of issue of the final completion certificate. During the
construction, the contractors demanded (2005) the release of performance
security on the grounds that as per Government orders (May 2003) the
provision for five per cent performance security was applicable on
percentage rate tenders only. The E-in-C decided (January 2006) to release
the performance security and accordingly, an amount of € 2.10 crore (shown

The action of the E-in-C was incorrect because the Government, in its orders

total deduction of 10 per cent already existed.

It was further seen that the contract of one package (KCP-3) was rescinded

had already been released. Thus premature release of performance security
and security deposit resulted in loss of ¥ 1.60 crore to the Government as

irregularly released to them. The CE Ganga Basin stated (December 2010)
that a show cause notice was being served to the EE and DAO and SAC.

Contrary to the provisions of the agreement in 31 cases of construction of the
Sihawal Canal, Keoti Canal, Purwa Canal and their distributories amounting
to = 133.40 crore, performance security amounting to = 5.74 crore was not
obtained from the contractor before signing (2006-07 to 2009-10) the
agreement. The contractors failed to achieve the work within the the
stipulated date (December 2007 to June 2010) and thus the performance of
the agreements was not secured.

The EE Lower Sihawal division, Churhat stated (September 2010) that as per
the E-in-C's orders, no performance security was required on item rate
contracts. The reply is not acceptable in view of the condition existing in the
agreement.

1.2.9.2 Excess payment to the contractor for drawing and design

Providing laying of the rising main of MS pipe of the Teonthar LIS was given (July
2008) to a supplier of Indore. The contract included mechanical works of % 27.44
crore on item rate basis and € nine crore for civil works on lump sum basis. As per the
agreement, the rates of mechanical works were inclusive of 10 per cent for drawing
and design of mechanical work only, payable on submission and approval of drawing
and design. The department, however, paid (August 2008 to March 2009) an amount
of = 3.64 crore (₹ 2.74 crore for mechanical works and ₹ 90 lakh for civil works) for
drawing and design.

33



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Scrutiny in audit revealed that as per the agreement, no payment for drawing and
design was payable on civil work. Therefore, payment of drawing and design of civil
work resulted in excess payment of 90 lakh to the supplier.

Further, the rates of pipes also included 10 per cent for drawing and designs which
were already paid (August 2008 and March 2009) to the supplier separately.
Therefore, the rates had to be reduced by 10 per cent, which was not done. Thus non-
reduction of the rates of pipes by 10 per cent resulted in excess payment to the
supplier off 2.74 crore.

The EE stated (August 2010) that the agency had submitted drawings and designs for
which payment of 10 per cent was made. The reply was not acceptable because as per the
agreement, the payment for drawings and. designs was notadmissible for civil works.

> Against the indent of SE, Bansagar Canal Circle, Rewa, MPLUN issued.
(March 2007 to April 2008) supply orders to a supplier of Nagpur for design,
supply and erection of MS pipes in seven super passages of Keoti and Purwa
canals and two MS pipe aqueducts on minors on the Shikarganj distributory.
As per the supply order, the firm was responsible for preparation of drawings
and designs and their approval from the department. The rates in the supply
order included 10 per cent for drawings and designs.

Scrutiny in audit revealed that the drawings and designs for the work were
prepared by the department. Therefore, the rates were required to be reduced
by 10 per cent. However, payment of € 7.13 crore was made to the supplier
between June 2007 and March 2010, without reducing the rates by 10 per
cent. Non-reduction of rates thus resulted in excess payment of 71.32 lakh
for work not done.

The EE, Distributory Division, Rewa stated (August 2010) that after
verification of the facts, the amount would be recovered from the supplier.
Audit, however, found (July 2010) that no amount against the firm was
payable with the division to recover the excess payment.

cent of quantities only to be paid at 75 per cent of the admissible rates.

1.2.10 Monitoring and Quality Control

The CE, Bansagar Project and E-in-C, WRD were responsible for monitoring the
progress of the project. They were not only responsible to ensure that the various
works of the project were executed as per the specified time schedule and sanctioned
cost but also to ensure the quality of execution by conducting the prescribed quality
tests at prescribed frequencies.

Instances of deficient monitoring and quality control were noticed in audit as
discussed below:

> Records pertaining to monitoring of quantities (for length of canal) and
quality of work during the period of 2005-10 were not maintained. Although
there were instances of large variations in the quantities of excavations and
cement concrete works, the required length of canal was not achieved. The
quality of work done in various cases was also found substandard and canal
specifications were not followed in the execution of works. As instructed by
the Principal Secretary, WRD, the Additional Secretary, WRD started
monitoring the progress of work from July 2010 onwards.

> The USR of the WR department required that compressive strength of
cement concrete should be tested at the frequency of four tests per 50 cu m of
concrete and thereafter, one test per 50 cu m of concrete. Accordingly, for
8908.75 cum of CC 1:3:6 and 6749.679 cu m of CC 1:2:4 executed under the
packages KCP-4 and PCP-2 of the Keoti canal division, Rewa and Purwa
canal division, Rewa respectively, against the requirement of 323 nos. of
tests, only 98 tests were conducted. Thus the quality control tests were not
done at the required frequencies to ensure the quality of works.

The EE stated (July 2010) that the quality control unit would be requested to
carry out frequent tests.

1.2.11 Conclusion

During the construction of Bansagar Project (Unit-I]) for the period of 2005-06 to
2009-10, inadequate survey led to large variations in quantities put to tender. Though
the Water Resources Department had no financial constraints, the canal construction
work was not completed within the stipulated date due to slow progress. Funds were
diverted for works which were outside the scope of the project. Irrigation potential
actually utilised was far beyond the irrigation potential created. Project execution
lacked synchronised execution of different project components like construction of
canals without head regulators and construction in patches with substantial gaps.
During the last three years, the department gave more emphasis on purchases than on
actual execution. Contract management at the field level was defective and as a
result, the specified progress was not achieved. Excess payments to contractors were
noticed. For construction of civil works, lump-sum contracts were used instead of
item rates, leading to payments at inflated rates. Performance securities /security
deposits were frequently released before completion of the works. The quality of
work was not monitored properly. Technical specifications for construction of the
canals were also not followed.

1.2.12 Recommendations

> Government should make effective rules regarding proper surveys and
estimates so as to avoid discrepancies between estimated quantities and
actual quantities.

> Funds should be utilised as per the Plans.

> The works should be strictly executed according to the Water Resources
Department's specifications and purchases should also be regulated
accordingly.

> Rules governing contract management should be followed strictly so as to
avoid excess payments/ undue benefit to the contractors.

> Monitoring and quality control system should be strengthened.

Chapter-I Performance Audit

Water Resources Department

1.3 Madhya Pradesh Water Sector Restructuring Project

Highlights

The Madhya Pradesh Water Sector Restructuring Project (MPWSRP) was
proposed (May 2004) by the Water Resources Department (WRD), Government of
Madhya Pradesh with an integrated vision for water sector development and its
management, associating line departments viz. Agriculture, Horticulture,
Livestock and Fisheries departments and various institutions. WRD received a
loan of = 1,919 crore from the World Bank towards MPWSRP. The progress of
project implementation, physical as well as financial, was very slow. There were
cases of delayed engagement of consultants, deficient implementation of the IT
system, poor financial management, non-achievement of targeted irrigation
potential, non-recovery of liquidated damages and advances, time and cost
overruns and undue benefits to contractors.

Some important findings of the performance audit of MPWSRP are given below:

Delays in engagement of consultants ranging from 13 to 44 months and delays in
delivery of services ranging from 15 to 35 months were the prime reasons for
delays in implementation of the entire project.

(Paragraph 1.3.6.1)

The progress during the years 2005-06, 2006-07 and 2007-08 was very slow. Out
of the project cost of 1,919 crore, only हैं 688.25 crore (38 per cent) was utilised
in five years against the project period of six years. Non-utilisation of funds led
to avoidable liability of commitment charges of € 8.26 crore.

(Paragraphs 1.3.7.2 and 1.3.7.3)

Against the targeted irrigation potential of 6.20 lakh hectares, only 0.69 lakh
hectares was created.

(Paragraph 1.3.8.2)

Non-conducting of core tests of cement concrete lining rendered payment of
% 26.65 crore, in four schemes, irregular.

(Paragraph 1.3.9.2)

Irregular award of work costing रैं 23.73 crore without invitation of open tenders
resulted in extra cost of हैं one crore.

(Paragraph 1.3.9.3)

37



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Additional security deposit of € 57.51 crore against 15 unbalanced bids was not
obtained and liquidated damages of< 20.93 crore were also not recovered.

(Paragraphs 1.3.10.1 and 1.3.10.3)

Irregular rejection of tenders in the first call resulted not only in
non-procurement of World Bank loan of f 28 crore but also in cost overrun by
% 4.61 crore.

(Paragraph1.3.10.8)

1.3.1 Introduction

The Madhya Pradesh Water Sector Restructuring Project (MPWSRP) was taken up
by the Water Resources Department (WRD), Government of Madhya Pradesh
(Government) with assistance from the World Bank (WB) in 2004 and with a target
of completion of March 2011.

WRD received (2004) a loan of € 1,919 crore from the WB towards MPWSRP with
an integrated vision for water sector development and its management, associating
line departments™ and various institutions* in the modernisation plan.

Five river basins” of Madhya Pradesh were selected for MPWSRP due to their
contiguity, high incidence of poverty, varied water management challenges and
presence of ageing irrigation schemes. The project was aimed at modernising the
existing irrigation and drainage assets in these basins covering about 6.20 lakh
hectares spread area. It included 654 (revised as 505 in 2009) schemes consisting of
five major, 33 medium and 616 minor schemes. A map showing the Project Basins in
which MPWSRP is being executed is given in Appendix 1.10.

1.3.2 Organisational set up

The Principal Secretary and the Secretary WRD are the administrative heads of the
department at the Government level. The overall planning, coordination,
procurement, implementation, financial control, monitoring and evaluation of
MPWSRP are done by the Project Director (PD), World Bank Project (WBP). Chief
Engineers (CEs), Superintending Engineers (SEs) and Executive Engineers (EEs) of
the concerned divisions of WRD assist him to implement the project. Apart from that,
the EEs are assisted by the line departments and other institutions. An Empowered
Committee (EC) headed by the Chief Secretary was formed in March 2005, to review
the progress of the MPWSRP at the apex level. The organogram of the department is
given in Appendix 1.11.

उ4. Linedepartments include Agriculture, Horticulture, Livestock and Fisheries departments.

36 Chambal Betwa (CB), Narmada Tapti (NT) , Dhasan Ken (DK), Yamuna Sindh, Tons and

Chapter-I Performance Audit

1.3.3, Audit Objectives

The performance audit was conducted with a view to ascertain whether:

> the planning was appropriate and in accordance with the World Bank
guidelines;

> adequate funds were available on time and were properly utilised;

> the execution of the project was carried out economically and efficiently;

> the project was completed within the stipulated time and improved water
resource management and irrigation service delivery; and

> an effective monitoring mechanism as well as a proper internal control

system were in place.

1.3.4 Audit criteria

The audit findings were based on the following criteria:
> Terms and conditions of the loan agreement with the WB.

> Madhya Pradesh Works Department Manual and irrigation specifications
issued by the Government.

> Contract documents, rules and orders issued by the Government.

> Indian Standard (IS) codes of practice, issued by Bureau of Indian Standards.

1.3.5 Scope and Methodology of Audit

The performance audit of the records of the project for the period from 2005-06 to
2009-10 was conducted during May to September 2010 and covered samples of three
major, four medium and six minor schemes being executed in 12 WRD divisions”
(out of 43 divisions) under two controlling offices. The schemes were selected
using the stratified random sampling method. Details of sample selection are
indicated in Appendix- 1.12.

An entry conference was held in April 2010 with the Principal Secretary, WRD and
Project Director (PD), WBP followed by an exit conference with the Additional
Secretary, WRD and the PD, WBP in December 2010.

Audit acknowledges the co-operation given by WRD during the course of
performance audit.

BVPP Bhopal.

Audit Findings

1.3.6 Planning

MPWSRP was aimed at restructuring and modernising the existing irrigation
schemes, assets and integration of line departments in the modernisation plan.
Special thrust was given to institutional strengthening of WRD to equip the project
with modern appropriate tools (through computerisations and communication
infrastructure i.e. LAN/WAN, decision support system etc.) and to have an effective
institutional coordination mechanism. As per the conditions of loan of the WB, WRD
prepared. a Project Implementation Plan (PIP) and Project Appraisal Document
(PAD) in 2004, which enshrined different strategies and milestones for appointment
of consultants and formation ofkey covenants for implementation of the MPWSRP.

Instances of non-adherence to the PIP leading to serious delays in engagement of
consultancy services and non-formation of two important key covenants noticed in
audit are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs:

1.3.6.1 Delay in engaging consultants

One of the vital components of the PIP was to engage consultants for implementation
of the entire project. The PIP envisaged engagement of 15 key consultants, who
would have a direct bearing on the successful implementation of the project.
Consultants were required to be engaged at the initial stage of the project as the work
of the consultants would ultimately support reforms in the WRD. Details regarding
the engagement of the consultants and their present status are shown in Appendix
1.13.

Audit found (August 2010) that:

> some of the works like topographic and cadastral survey, preparation of the
detailed project report {termed as Scheme Modernisation Plan, (SMP)} etc
which were required to be done at the very beginning of the project actually
started after delays of about 20 to 34 months;

> 10 consultants were engaged after delays ranging from 13 to 44 months with
reference to the milestones specified in the PIP;

> four consultants delayed delivery of the services for periods ranging from 15
to 35 months vis-a-vis the milestones specified in the PIP. One consultant was
terminated by WBP.

Delays in deliverables (like survey and preparation of SMPs) and under-
performance by the consultants affected the overall performance of the project.
Delay in one deliverable induced delay in other activities as these were interrelated.
and resulted in the overall delays in the implementation of the project.

40



Chapter-I Performance Audit

During the exit conference, the PD, WBP stated (December 2010) that due to multi-
stage evaluation by the WB, the entire process took a long time. He also stated that
WRD had no experience in implementing WBP. Therefore, the delay was beyond
control due to adherence to the WB procurement process and its conditions.

> three important consultants for 'Technical Examination of Works’, ‘Internal
Audit' and ‘Monitoring and Learning activity’, required to be engaged during
the first 18 months (December 2005) from implementation of the project had
still not been engaged (December 2010). Engagement of two consultants for
‘Research Programmes on Reservoir Fish Production Enhancement’ and
‘Improved Supplementary Feeds and Feedings' was deferred by the WB.

Due to non-engagement of consultants for 'Technical Examination of Works' and
‘Monitoring and Learning Activity’, the quality issues remained unassured and
continuous assessment of the efficiency and impact of the project in achieving the
desired objectives was not done.

The PD, WBP stated (December 2010) during the exit conference that the
engagement of consultants for 'Technical Examination of works' and ‘Monitoring
and Learning activity' were in the final stage. The WB process took a long time in
deciding terms of references which culminated in the overall delay.

The reply is not acceptable as the engagement of consultants at the end of the project
period would not serve the purpose.

> the agreements did not provide penalty for delay in delivery of services and

under-performance by consultants to ensure strict adherence of the
prescribed time schedule.

The department stated (December 2010) that it was binding on WRD to adhere to the
WB approved documents, where there was no such penalty clause.

The reply is not acceptable as due to non-inclusion of the penalty clause for under-
performance, strict adherence of the prescribed time schedule could not be ensured.
This also contributed to time over-runs and cost over-runs of the project.

1.3.6.2 Non-formation of key covenants

As per the conditions of the Project Appraisal Document (PAD), WRD had to
establish 14 key covenants” by December 2005. Audit found (August 2010) that the
department has formed (December 2005) 12 key covenants while the two key
covenants given below had not yet been formed.

> the State Water Tariff Regulatory Commission (SWTRC),

> the installation and testing of computer systems and communication
infrastructure (WAN/LAN) for both data and voice

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

The department stated (December 2010) that SWTRC was to be formed by a
legislation of the State Assembly and Wide Area Network (WAN) was suspended to
avoid duplication as the State was developing the State Wide Area Network
(SWAN).

1.3.7 Financial Management

As per the PIP, the objective of the financial management system was to enhance the
accountability of the project management system, provide reliable and timely
financial information, timely preparation and submission of Statements of
Expenditure (SOEs) and Financial Monitoring Reports (FMRs). Proper allotment
and expenditure by WRD and other departments was necessary to avoid under-
utilisation of funds and payment of interest on unspent amounts.

1.3.7.1 Fund flow

The flow of funds through the WB loan is based on eligible expenditure that qualifies
for reimbursement. The project receives the funds from the WB through Government
of India (GOT) which is subsequently disbursed to various implementing agencies
(IAs). The basis of reimbursement from the WB is the SOEs that are prepared and
submitted on a monthly basis by the WBP. The loan off 1919 crore for the praject was
approved by the WB in 2004 which was to be repaid in 20 years. The loan agreement
included penalty (termed as commitment charge) for under-utilisation of the loan.

A few instances of deficiencies in management of funds are discussed below:
1.3.7.2 Short utilisation of funds

Allotment of funds and expenditure on the project for the last five years ending
March 2010 revealed that as against the committed distribution of funds of
& 1789.98 crore, the department incurred an expenditure of only € 688.25 crore as
shown in Table No.1.16.

> the expenditure incurred against the committed distribution of funds for the

> the original allotments were reduced in all the five years due to non-finalisation

the first two years. In the succeeding three years, it was due to slow
processing of SMPs, delays in tendering and awarding of civil works and
non-execution/delayed execution of rehabilitation works.

1.3.7.3 Avoidable liabilities towards commitment charges

As per Section 2.05 of Article-II of the loan agreement with the WB, the borrower
was to pay a commitment charge at three fourth of one per cent per annum on the
principal amount of the loan not withdrawn as per the agreed schedule.

Scrutiny of allotments and expenditure for the last five years revealed that the total
expenditure was as low as 38.45 per cent of the committed distribution of funds. This
demonstrated poor financial management on the part of the WBP, leading to
accumulation of avoidable liability of payment of commitment charges of % 8.26
crore as shown in Table No. 1.16.

The department stated (December 2010) that delay in clearance of SMPs by the WB
was the biggest bottleneck in ensuring timely utilisation of funds.

1.3.7.4 Under-utilisation of funds by the line departments

Scrutiny of allotment and expenditure for the last five years revealed under-utilisation
of funds (approximately 50 per cent) by the line departments which defeated the
process of integration of the line departments into the modernisation plan. The
expenditure incurred against the committed distribution of funds for the last five years
was as low as7.3 per cent in the year 2005-06 to 74.95 per cent in the year 2008-09.

The Deputy Director (Agriculture) stated in May 2010 that due to non-fixation of
targets for the year 2009-10 andnon-receipt of allotments in time, the funds could not
be utilised. The Deputy Director(s), Veterinary and Fisheries stated (May 2010) that
the allotted funds remained unutilised due to shortage of staff and non-receipt of
sanctions from the higher authorities.

The department further stated (December 2010) that the line departments had requested.
WEP for arevised financial disbursement system for better utilisation of funds.

Under MPWSRP, 654 irrigation schemes were taken up for modernisation and

delivery and drainage assets, creation and. utilisation of irrigation potential,
