to time, the latest proposal being one sent in May 2010. The reply is not convincing
as the building was constructed without properly assessing the feasibility of the
diagnostic centre at Bhanpura and the equipment were purchased without even
sanctioning the requisite staff.

Thus, the injudicious decision to set up a diagnostic centre at Bhanpura and not provide
requisite staffled to idle expenditure of® 1.98 croreon building and equipment.

The matter was referred to the Government (June 2010). Their reply had not been
received (November 2010).

Water Resources Department

2.4.2 Idleinvestment and non-recovery of additional security deposit

Exhibition of inadequate quantities in the agreement as against the technically
sanctioned quantities resulted in idle investment of = 81.19 lakh and loss of
% 38.26 lakh due to non-retention of additional security deposit of a contractor.

The Madhya Pradesh Works Department Manual (Manual) states that tenders for
works should be invited after accord of administrative approval (AA), technical
sanction (TS) and sanction to detailed estimates by the competent authorities. The

83



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

schedule of quantities in the tender document should truly represent the quantities
mentioned in the TS. Further, clause 3.28 of the tender document provides that ifthe
tenderer has quoted disproportionately high rates for some items, or the tender is
unbalanced”, the payment of such items shall be limited to the estimated rates plus or
minus the overall percentage. The balance payment of such items shall be retained as
additional security deposit, which shall be released only after completion of the
entire work. In case of failure on the part of the contractor to complete the work for
any reason, the entire additional security deposit shall be forfeited.

Technical sanction of € 1.35 crore for construction of the Gawlan high level bridge
was accorded (September 2007) by the Superintending Engineer (SE), WR circle,
Khargone. The lowest item rate tender of a contractor at € 1.08 crore (i.e, 10.61 per
cent above unified schedule of rates (USR) - July 2007) was accepted (February
2008) by the Chief Engineer (CE), Narmada Tapti Basin, Indore. The work order
issued (February 2008) by the EE, WR Division, Khargone, stipulated 12 months
(including the rainy season) for completion of the work. The contractor, however,
stopped (November 2008) the work midway after executing a work of f 94.19 lakh.
The division made payment of the fourth and final bill of हैं 81.19 lakh to the
contractor in July 2009.

Audit scrutiny (July 2009) revealed that although the TS for the work was for® 1.35
crore, the estimated quantities of some major items of works (viz. quantities of
teinforced cement concrete for piers and steel reinforcement) included in the TS
were not included fully in the schedule of quantities of the tender document as shown
in Appendix 2.9. The tenders were invited with the probable amount of contract of
only हैं 98.01 lakh instead of € 1.35 crore. As a result, after providing and laying
cement concrete for the foundation and piers upto ‘plus 10 per cent' of the agreed.
quantities, the contractor stopped the works and expressed (November 2008) his
inability to execute the remaining work at the agreed rates. Thus the quantities put to
tender was not commensurate with the estimated requirement of the work, which
resulted in incomplete work. As of January 2009, the physical progress of the piers
was 50 to 60 per cent of the designed height and the subsequent construction of the
deck slab and the ridding surface of the bridge was not executed by the contractor.
The SE directed (February 2009) the EE to foreclose the contract and release the
entire dues of the contractor.

Neither had any departmental investigation been conducted nor had any
responsibility been fixed for inviting tenders with the incomplete schedule of
quantities. No action was taken (November 2009) to complete the incomplete work.

2.5 Regulatory issues and other points

Forest Department

2.5.1 Non/short realisation of net present value

Net present value amounting हैं 6.63 crore was short realised from five agencies
towards diversion of 528 hectares of forest land for non-forest use.

The Government of Madhya Pradesh, Forest Department had prescribed (September
2008) rates for calculating the net present value (NPV) to be charged from the user
agencies for forest land diverted for non-forest use. The rates were in accordance
with the type and density of the forest (minimum हैं 5.80 lakh per hectare and
maximum 9.20 lakh per hectare)”.

20 As per guidelines issued vide No 5-1/98-FC (PT-Il) dated 18 September 2003 issued by Government of
India, Ministry of Environment and Forest.

Test check of records of six Divisional Forest Officers’ (DFO) and the

528.016 hectare of forest land under jurisdiction of six divisions and one national
park were diverted for non-forestry use for various projects”. However, NPV
amounting to हैं 30.52 crore was realised against the recoverable amount of 37.15
crore. This resulted in non/short recovery of = 6.63 crore of NPV from the user
agencies.

On this being pointed out in audit, the DFOs and the Field Director, Panna National
Park, stated (between July and October 2009), that the amount would be recovered
from the respective user agencies.

The matter was reported to the Principal Chief Conservator of Forest and the
Government (April 2010). The Government replied (October 2010) that the
remaining amount would be recovered.

General

2.5.2 Failure to enforce accountability and protect the interests of the
Government

[A] Government response on Inspection Reports and Paragraphs
The Principal Accountant General (Civil and Commercial Audit), Madhya Pradesh,
Gwalior (PAG) and the Accountant General (Works and Receipt Audit), Madhya
Pradesh, Bhopal (AG) conduct periodical audit of the Government departments to
test check, inter-alia, the transactions and verify the maintenance of important
accounting and other records as per prescribed rules and procedures. Irregularities
detected during audit are reported through Inspection Reports (IRs) to ensure
tectificatory action in compliance of the prescribed rules and procedures and
accountability for the deficiencies and lapses. As per the Regulations” on Audit and
Accounts, the Heads of Offices and next higher authorities are required to comply
with the observations contained in the IRs, promptly rectify the defects/ omissions
and report their compliance to the PAG/AG within four weeks of their receipt. The
PAG/AG also brings serious irregularities to the notice of the Heads of Departments.
Half-yearly reports of pending IRs and paras are sent to the concerned Principal
Secretaries/ Secretaries of the departments to facilitate monitoring of compliance to
the audit observations in the pending IRs. Besides, annual public statements
regarding pending audit observations are also to be made by the heads of
departments.

21 DFO (G) East Mandla, Vidisha, Raisen, Rajgarh, North Seoni, Narsinghpur.

22 Idea Cellular Ltd. Indore, Mudiya Khera reservoir, National Highway, Rani Avantibai Lodhi
Sagar Pariyojna and Mining Project.

23 Regulations on Audit and Accounts framed by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India
(As notified in the Gazette of India on November 20, 2007).

outstanding as on 31 August 2010. This included 10,526 paragraphs of 5,112 IRs

The heads of offices whose records were audited and the heads of departments did

initiate action with respect to the defects, omissions and irregularities pointed out in
them. The Principal Secretaries/Secretaries of the departments who were informed
of the position through half-yearly reports also did not ensure that the concerned
offices of the Department take prompt and timely action.

Absence of any action against the defaulting officers facilitated the continuance of
irregularities and losses to the Government despite these being pointed out in audit. It
is recommended that Government should look into the procedure for fixing
responsibility of the officials who failed to take corrective/remedial action on the
audit observations and failed to send replies to IRs/paragraphs within a prescribed
time. Action should be initiated to recover losses, outstanding advances, over-
payments, etc. in a time-bound manner and enforce accountability to ensure proper
and timely response to the issues brought out in audit.

As per instructions issued (November 1998) by the Madhya Pradesh Vidhan Sabha
Secretariat, the departments are to intimate the Public Accounts Committee (PAC)
and the PAG/AG about the action taken or any action proposed by them in respect of
Audit Report paragraphs within three months of presentation of the Audit Report in
the State Legislature.

(August 2010) awaited as per details given in Appendix-2.15.

[C] Government response on PAC's recommendations

The Chief Secretary, Government of Madhya Pradesh issued (November 1994)
instructions to all the departments to intimate the PAC about the action taken or the
action proposed by them in respect of PAC's recommendations within six months of
presentation of PAC's report in the State Legislature.

Chapter-III Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

CHAPTER III

Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

Farmers Welfare and Agriculture Development Department

3.1 Integrated Audit of Farmers Welfare and Agriculture
Development Department

Highlights

The Farmers Welfare and Agriculture Development Department plays a vital role
in the socio-economic development of the State. For increasing production and
productivity of major crops, the department implemented various crap
development schemes. However, the department failed to achieve the targets of
production as envisaged in the Tenth and Eleventh Plans. Budgetary and
expenditure controls were weak and deficiencies were noticed in cash
management. Field monitoring on demonstrations, distribution of seeds, seed
minikits and selection of beneficiaries by district officers were almost absent. Soil
surveys were not conducted and soil testing was far below the targets. Performance
of quality testing laboratories was poor.

Some of the important audit findings are as follows:

Supplementary provisions of = 42.35 crore obtained during 2005-10 proved
unnecessary.

(Paragraph 3.1.7.4)

Scheme funds amounting to हैं 133.09 crore were lying in the bank accounts at
various levels and a total unutilised amount of € 28.15 crore was lying with other
agencies/departments.

(Paragraphs 3.1.7.8, 3.1.7.9, 3.1.7.10, 3.1.7.12 and 3.1.7.13)

The department failed to achieve the targets of production of foodgrains set in
the Tenth and Eleventh plans.

(Paragraph 3.1.8.1)

Field officers did not ensure that foundation/certified seeds and seed minikits
were distributed well before the cut off dates and that the benefits reached the
targeted beneficiaries. Adequate attention was not paid to field demonstrations.

(Paragraphs 3.1.8.2, 3.1.8.3, 3.1.8.4, 3.1.9.5 and 3.1.9.6)

89



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Performance of Government agriculture farms was poor and 31 to 49 per cent
(Kharif season) and 37 to 54 per cent (Rabi season) cultivable area was not
utilised.

(Paragraph 3.1.8.5)

Expenditure of f 5.06 crore remained unfruitful on construction of Khet Taals
and Balram Taals as the farmers left the works incomplete and did not follow
the prescribed standards in construction of Taals.

(Paragraph 3.1.8.6)

Sub-standard seeds, fertilizers and pesticides were sold to cultivators. The
prescribed ratio of 4:2:1 in consumption of nitrogen, phosphate and potassium
was not maintained.

(Paragraphs 3.1.10.2 and 3.1.10.3)

Soil surveys were not conducted and there were substantial shortfalls in soil
testing.

(Paragraphs 3.1.10.4 and 3.1.10.5)

Shortage of technical staff (57 per cent) affected the implementation of
programme. Poor man-power management led to irregular posting in other
department and excess staff over the sanctioned strength.

(Paragraphs 3.1.11.1, 3.1.11.2 and 3.1.11.3)

Internal audit was absent as only 39 offices were covered out of 308 offices by the
Director.

(Paragraph 3.1.13.2)

3.1.1 Introduction

The Farmers Welfare and Agriculture Development (FW&AD) Department plays a
vital role in the socio-economic development of the State. The objectives of the
department are to improve the production and productivity of all crops by means of
soil and water management, minor irrigation programmes, farm mechanisation,
dissemination of latest technologies among cultivators and ensuring availability of
improved/quality inputs. For the purpose, the department implements 14 State Plan
and 19 Central sector/Centrally sponsored schemes including schemes under macro-
management.

Madhya Pradesh has a geographical area of 307.55 lakh hectares (ha). The
agriculture cropped area as on 31 March 2009 was 190.94 lakh ha and the net sown
area was 150.44 lakh ha (79 per cent) including irrigated area of 65.06 lakh ha (34 per
cent). The main crops of the State are wheat, paddy, maize, jowar, millets (cereals),
gram, arhar, urad, moong (pulses), soyabean, mustard, groundnut (oilseeds),
sugarcane, cotton (commercial crops) ete.

90



Chapter-III Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

3.1.2 Organisational Set-up

The department is headed by a Principal Secretary and has two separate Directorates,
viz. (i) Farmers Welfare and Agriculture Development and (ii) Agriculture
Engineering at State level. The organogram of the department is given in Appendix 3.1,

3.1.3 Audit Objectives

The audit objectives were to assess whether:
> the planning of the department was effective;
> the financial management was efficient, effective and economical;

> the schemes were implemented effectively and quality controls were
adequate;

> the human resource management was appropriate in implementation of
programmes/activities and inventory controls were adequate; and

> the monitoring mechanism and internal audit were effective.

3.1.4 Audit criteria

Audit findings were benchmarked against the following criteria :

> Provisions of the Madhya Pradesh Treasury Code (MPTC) and the Madhya
Pradesh Financial Code (MPFC).

> Targets set in Tenth and Eleventh Five Year Plans/Annual Action Plans.

> Guidelines of State and Centrally Sponsored Schemes and orders/directives
issued from time to time by Government of India (GOI) and the State
Government (Government).

> Departmental manuals.

3.1.5 Audit Coverage and Methodology

The integrated audit was conducted during February to December 2010 covering the
period 2005-10. The records of offices of two directorates and subordinate offices in
13 districts' were selected for test check by using the Simple Random Sampling
Without Replacement (SRSWOR) method. Entry conferences were held with the
Director, FW&AD and Director, Agriculture Engineering in February and July 2010
tespectively in which audit objectives, audit criteria and audit coverage were
discussed. An exit conference was held on 9" December 2010 with the Principal
Secretary to the Government of Madhya Pradesh, FW&AD department. The results
of discussion have been incorporated in the Report at appropriate places.

I Balaghat, Betul, Bhopal, Burhanpur, Chhatarpur, Dindori, Gwalior, Jhabua,
Mandsaur,Panna, Ratlam, Shahdol and Shivpuri.

91



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Audit Findings

3.1.6 Planning

The Annual Plan for the State as well as the district level are prepared on the basis of
the ceilings of expenditure fixed for the department by the State Planning
Commissioner each year. In the State level Annual Plan, the targets for production of
major crops are proposed at the directorate level, keeping in view the targets of the
Five Year Plans, targets and achievements of production of the previous years and
priorities fixed by GOI/Government. However, instead of fixing the targets for
districts on the basis of targets projected in the State level Plan, district-wise targets
of production were fixed each year in the meetings held at the divisional level under
the chairmanship of the Agriculture Production Commissioner before the
commencement of the kharifand rabi seasons, which were actually followed by the
districts. As a result, there was considerable mismatch between the targets projected
in State level Annual Plan and the targets actually followed by the districts as shown
in Table 3.1 below:

Table 3.1: Variations in Annual Plan targets and targets actually followed
(In lakh tonnes)

Year Targets for Food grains Targets for Oilseeds

districts

2009-10 172.97 161.80 70.97 72.80

(Source: Annual Plan and Departmental Figures)

Thus, the targets set in the State level Annual Plan were not being followed strictly
and the Annual Plan remained open for modification twice a year.

3.1.7.2 Budget estimates

Rule 192 of MPFC provides that budget estimates (BEs) should be furnished to the
Finance Department (FD) before the dates fixed by the FD through the
administrative department. It was, however, noticed that the department sent the BEs
to the FD with delays ranging between 30 to 75 days from the dates prescribed by the
FD during 2005-10. The Director, FW&AD attributed the delays to late receipt of
BEs from the Drawing and Disbursing Officers (DDOs). At the exit conference, the
department accepted the audit observation and assured compliance in future.

3.1.7.3 Unrealistic budgeting

The State Budget Manual provides that the BEs should be as close and accurate as
possible. Audit observed that during 2005-10, provisions were made on ad hoc basis
without proper analysis of the expenditure trend of the previous years as shown in
Table 3.4.

The State Institute of Agriculture Extension and Trainings (SIAET) Barkhedikalan,
Bhopal was declared an autonomous body by the Government of Madhya Pradesh
during 2007-08 and grant-in-aid was to be paid to the institute for day to day
functioning of the institute. Scrutiny of records of the Director, FW&AD revealed
that grants-in-aid of % 1.68 crore and हैं 1.66 crore were paid during 2008-09 and
2009-10 respectively against which the institute incurred expenditure of € 0.75 crore
and & 1.17 crore. As per conditions of the sanction order issued (April 2008),
utilisation certificates of funds were to be submitted before submission of the next
year's estimates. Audit observed that while providing grant-in-aid for the year 2009-
10, the unspent balance of 2008-09 was not adjusted. As a result, = 1.42 crore (% 0.93
crore for 2008-09 + 0.49 crore for 2009-10) was lying unspent in the bank account
of SIAET. At the exit conference, the department admitted the excess release of
grant.

schemes were not implemented fully as planned. During the exit conference, the

3.1.7.11 Cash Management

Rule 53 of MPTC envisages that the officer in-charge of the cash book should
physically verify the cash balance at the end of each month. Fortnightly verification
of drawals made from treasury should be conducted by DDOs with reference to
treasury records. Temporary advances paid to officials should be adjusted as soon as
possible and in no case should be left unadjusted beyond three months.

Test check of records of 37 DDOs in selected districts and in the Directorates
tevealed that DDOs disregarded the provisions relating to cash management as
discussed below:

> Cash balances in the cash books were not found verified physically regularly
at the end ofeach month by 10 DDOs.

> Fortnightly verifications of drawals with reference to treasury records were
not done by 26 DDOs.

4 Directorate of Veterinary Service® 30.92 crore, Directorate of Fisheries % 10.01 crore and

Directorate of Horticulture and Farm Forestry® 15.52 crore.

97



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

> Temporary advances amounting to हैं 88.98 lakh for the period 1977-2010
were lying unadjusted as of November 2010, out of which 1.79 lakh was
outstanding for the last 10 to 32 years in the Directorate, FW&AD. DDOs
stated that action for adjustment of the advances was under process.

> Reconciliation of balances of cash books with balances of bank pass books
was not done by 15 DDOs which resulted in discrepancy of € 5.94 crore
between the figures appearing in the cash books and the pass books as of
March 2010. Four DDOs did not get the pass books completed from the
banks.

> Monthly review of the Bill Register was not done by 19 DDOs and review of
the Bill Transit Book twice a week was not done by 22 DDOs as required
under Rules 197 and. 276 of MPTC respectively.

> Security deposits were not obtained from the cashiers/storekeepers by 17
DDOsas required under provisions of Rule 282 of MPFC.

> Bank draft registers were not found prepared by nine DDOs in the format
prescribed in the MPTC.

DDOs accepted the audit observations and assured to follow the codal provisions in
future. During the exit conference, the department stated that instructions would be
issued to observe the codal provisions.

3.1.7.13 Parking of funds with agencies

(i) The department was required to monitor the physical and financial progress of
works being executed by the agency for their completion within the stipulated time
frame. Scrutiny of records of the Directorate, FW&AD revealed that under the
scheme ATMA, & 2.40 crore was approved (September 2009) by GOI under district
level innovative activities during 2009-10. The Director, FW&AD paid (September
2009 to March 2010) हैं 2.40 crore to the Madhya Pradesh State Cooperative
Marketing Federation (Markfed) for renovation of 19 district level training centres.
Out of these 19 training centres, the works of seven training centres were not started,
the works of seven training centres were incomplete and the work of only one
training centre, i.e. Betul was completed. The status of four training centres (Ujjain,
Sheopur kalan, Indore and Sagar) was not available with the Directorate. Out of
₹ 2.40 crore, only ₹ैं 0.30 crore was utilised and the remaining amount of € 2.10 crore
was outstanding with Markfed. The Director accepted the audit contention and
assured to obtain the progress of work from Markfed.

(ii) To ensure availability of seeds of wheat and pulses under NFSM, Director,
FW&AD directed (June 2008) the Deputy Directors to make advance payment to
Madhya Pradesh Beej Farm Vikas Nigam during 2008-09. Against the advance
payment of = 3.69 crore paid to the agency, seeds costing = 1.18 crore were not
received in 15 districts.

Atthe exit conference, the department assured to take action in this regard.

3.1.8.2 Seed production and distribution

Breeder seeds are multiplied into foundation seeds and subsequently into certified
seeds. The requirement of breeder seeds was indented by the Director, FW&AD
from GOI based on the demand of seed producing agencies and Government
agriculture farms.

>

Vv

The shortfalls during 2005-10 under various schemes in procurement of
breeder seeds ranged from five to 93 per cent, production of foundation seeds
from three to 94 per cent, production of certified seeds from six to 99 per cent
and distribution of certified seeds from seven to 100 per cent as given in
Appendix 3.2. The Director FW&AD attributed the shortfalls to non-
availability of seeds, adverse weather conditions and non-conduction of seed
production programmes by district level officers. Shortage of seeds, which
were a vital input, adversely affected the agricultural production.

Subsidy was admissible on seeds of latest varieties notified within 10 years. It
was observed that subsidy of € 34.17 lakh on 6973.13 quintal of seeds was
paid by 11 Deputy Directors, Agriculture (DDAs) during 2006-10 under the
Integrated Cereal Development Programme (ICDP) on varieties of seeds
which were more than 10 years old. DDAs stated that distribution was made
on the basis of availability of seeds and demand of farmers. This led to
payment of subsidy on inadmissible seeds.

The seed was to be distributed to the beneficiaries before the prescribed dates
given for the Kharif and Rabi seasons. Scrutiny of records by Audit in
selected districts revealed that in six districts, 3628.84 quintal seeds were
provided to Rural Agricultural Extension Officers (RAEOs) under the
schemes Surajdhara, Annapurna and Seed Village scheme with delays
tanging between 16 and 69 days during 2008-10. DDAs stated that seed
supplying agencies failed to supply the seed in time.

At the exit conference, the department accepted the audit observation and stated that
instructions would be issued to DDAs to follow the scheme guidelines.

3.1.8.6 Water resource management-Expenditure on incomplete Taals (Ponds)

With a view to conserving rain water in farmers' fields for irrigation purposes, the
Khet Taal scheme was launched in 2006-07 by the Government for all categories of
farmers. The farmers had to choose any of the three models prescribed in the scheme
for which assistance of 50 per cent of the cost of ponds, limited to हैं 16,350 per pond,
was to be paid in two instalments to the identified farmers. The first instalment was to
be paid after completion of 50 per cent of the work and the second instalment after
completion of work within three months of getting the first instalment (excluding the
rainy season). The construction of inlets and outlets in the ponds was compulsory in
the scheme. The scheme was closed from 2008-09. Entries in the measurement book
were essential at the time of valuation of work before making payment to the
beneficiaries.

Scrutiny of records of 11 test-checked Assistant Soil Conservation Officers
(ASCOs) revealed that in 1,015 cases, the Taals were not found completed as
per the size of the models selected by the farmers to whom the first instalment
of हैं 68.53 lakh was paid. Since the farmers failed to construct the standard
size, the minimum water holding capacity of the Taals required for irrigation
could not be ensured in the test-checked ASCOs. Thus the objectives of the
scheme were not fulfilled.

Assistance of हैं 1.31 crore was paid in full by six ASCOs to 982 farmers who
had not constructed the inlets and outlets in their ponds.

Vv

> During test check of records of six ASCOs, it was noticed that 252 Taals
under RKVY for which हैं 36.40 lakh was paid as first instalment remained
incomplete during 2008-09. As a result, the Taals were not constructed up to
the standard size and the expenditure incurred remained unfruitful.

The ASCOs stated that farmers did not take interest in completing the work to the
standard size. At the exit conference, the department agreed with the audit
observations.

The Balram Taal scheme was launched in 2007-08 by the Government to cover more
irrigated area than the Khet Tal scheme, through the construction of Taals. The
farmers had to choose any of the four models prescribed in the scheme for which
assistance of 50 per cent (small and marginal farmers), 40 per cent (other farmers)
and 75 per cent (SC/ST farmers) of the cost of ponds limited to हैं 1,00,000 (SC/ST)
and 80,000 (other farmers) was to be paid in two instalments to selected farmers on
the pattern of Khet Taal scheme. The farmers had to follow the standard size, depth
and slope of the model as prescribed in the scheme. As per provisions contained in the
scheme, the construction of inlets and outlets in the Taals was compulsory for
farmers.

farmers legally bound to complete the work after getting the first instalments.
Consequently, farmers left the work incomplete and the expenditure on the
incomplete work remained unfruitful. During the exit conference, the department
assured to examine the cases and to take corrective measures.

3.1.9.1 Support to State Extension Programmes for Extension Reforms
Scheme

The scheme ‘Support to State Extension Programmes for Extension Reforms' was
launched in 2005-06 by GOI to operationalise agricultural extension reforms across
the country. The objectives of the scheme were to bring reforms in agricultural
extension services, technology dissemination and development in agricultural
technology. The resources required for the scheme were to be shared between the
Central and the State in the ratio of 90:10. Ten per cent of the State's share included
contribution of the State, beneficiary contribution and/or the contribution of non-
governmental organisation. A cafeteria” of activities categorised separately for State
and district levels were to be undertaken in the scheme. Expenses on district level
activities were not to exceed the ceilings fixed in the cafeteria and10 per cent
contribution was to be realised from beneficiaries with respect to beneficiary
oriented activities at the district level revised subsequently (July 2007) to five per
cent for SC, ST and women farmers.

Review of implementation of the scheme revealed the following irregularities:

Shortfalls were The targets fixed for various major activities under the ATMA cafeteria could not be

implementation between 26 and 100 per cent. The Director, FW&AD attributed the shortfall to
project elections of Lok Sabha, State Assembly (2008-09) and local bodies elections (2009-
activities. 10). Scrutiny of records of test-checked DDAs revealed that the non-achievement of

targets was due to shortage of staff, frequent revision/late receipt of targets and

late/non-release of funds according to targets. At the exit conference, the department

accepted the audit observation.

> Expenditure of & 5.35 crore was incurred in excess of ceilings applicable for
district level activities at State level. In nine test checked districts",
expenditure of हैं 1.27 crore was incurred more than the ceiling by DDAs.

3.1.9.2 National Food Security Mission

With a view to increase production of rice, wheat and pulses through area expansion
and productivity enhancement in a sustainable manner in the identified districts, the
National Food Security Mission (NFSM) was launched in 2007-08 in the State. The
scheme had three components viz. NFSM-rice, NFSM-wheat and NFSM-pulses.
The objectives of the scheme were to restore soil fertility and increase productivity at
the individual farm level besides creation of employment opportunities. Under the
scheme, nine districts for rice, 30 districts for wheat and 20 districts for pulses were
identified. Review of implementation of the scheme revealed the following
irregularities:

A Project Management Team (PMT) consisting of technical experts was to be
constituted at the State and the district level for technology transfer and
implementation of the Mission. The PMT was required to provide the
technical service/advice for which experts (consultants and technical
assistants) were to be hired on contract. Against the requirement of 64
consultants and 202 technical assistants, only 23 consultants and 98 technical
assistants were working. At the exit conference, the department accepted the
audit observation and assured filling up of vaccant posts.

3.1.9.3 Seed Village Scheme

The Seed Village scheme (a Central sector scheme) was launched in 2005-06 with
the objective of enhancing crop production/productivity through distribution of
foundation/certified seeds and providing training on seed production and seed
technology to selected farmers of identified villages. As per a provision of the
scheme, training was to be provided to a group of 50 to 150 farmers for three days.
Initially, one day training was to be given to farmers at the time of sowing of seed
crops. Subsequently, training for one day was to be organised during flower initiation
stage of the seed crop and concluding one day training was to be provided after
harvest and at the time of seed processing to impart knowledge on seed cleaning,
seed grading, seed storage and packing aspects etc.

Scrutiny of records in selected districts revealed that in 10 districts”, 1454.41 MT
seeds of different crops was distributed to 1,03,962 farmers during 2005-10 but the
required training was not provided to 45,583 (44 per cent) farmers. As a result, the
object of imparting training as envisaged in the scheme could not be achieved. At the
exit conference, the department stated that training was not provided due to paucity
of funds. The reply is not convincing as funds were available in the scheme which
were notutilised by the Director, FW&AD.

3.1.9.4 Integrated Cereal Development Programme

The Integrated Cereal Development Programme (ICDP) (rice/wheat) was
withdrawn in the NFSM-rice and NFSM-wheat identified districts as per the
instructions issued by GOI (August 2007) and by the Government (November 2007).
Further, the pattern of assistance under various components of ICDP was revised.
(July 2008) by GOI. Audit, however, noticed that Director, FW&AD during 2008-
09, fixed the targets as per the old ICDP pattern and released budget allotment of
%8.75 crore under ICDP to districts for implementation of the programme. In six test-
checked districts, it was noticed that both the components ICDP-wheat/rice and
NFSM-wheat/rice were implemented in total disregard of the above instructions.
Thus, expenditure of € 98.63 lakh incurred during 2007-09 under ICDP-wheat/rice
was irregular. During the exit conference, the department assured to examine the
matter and to take necessary action.

3.1.9.5 Integrated Scheme of Oilseed, Pulse, Oilpalm and Maize

As per orders (July and October 2001) of the Government, certified seed was to be
distributed at subsidised rates to the beneficiaries selected by RAEOs and approved
by the Krishi Sthai Samitis of Janpad Panchayats and sent to cooperative societies for

farmers of SC/ST to adopt cultivation of high productive crops of oilseeds and

farmers to adopt high productive cereal crops. As per orders (May and July 2000) of

to beneficiaries selected by Rural Agricultural Extension Officers (RAEOs) and
approved by Janapad Panchayats. The bills showing the actual cost of seeds
distributed to farmers were to be verified by the SADOs and sent to DDAs for
payment of the assistance to seed supplying agencies. Physical verification of
beneficiaries was to be conducted at different levels at prescribed percentages fixed.
for different officials. A register showing detailed information of farmers was to be
maintained by the RAEOs. The Sub-divisional Agriculture Officers, during the time

3.1.10 Quality Control

3.1.10.1 Quality Control of Agricultural Inputs

To ensure availability of good quality inputs (seeds, fertilizers and pesticides)
government established one seed laboratory (Gwalior), four fertilizer laboratories”
and one pesticide laboratory (Jabalpur) in the State under the Seed Act 1966,
Fertilizer Control Order 1985 and Pesticide Act respectively. GOI issued (August
2007) the recommendations of the National Conference of Agriculture for kharif
campaign 2007 in which it was directed to strengthen the seed testing system in the
State by using modern technology and by establishing one seed-testing laboratory in
each district. Audit, however, observed that only one seed-testing laboratory was
being run by the department. The Government nominated SADOs as inspectors to
ensure the enforcement of provisions prescribed by GOI under these Acts and orders
on seeds, fertilizers and pesticides. As per targets fixed, inspectors were required to
take samples of seeds (27500), fertilizers (33300) and pesticides (7500) during 2005-
10. Against these targets, 19896 samples of seeds, 24,681 samples of fertilizers and
5650 samples of pesticides were drawn by the inspectors, resulting in shortfall of
targets by 28 per cent (seeds) and 26 per cent (fertilizers) and 25 per cent (pesticides)
as shown in Appendix 3.5. Apart from the shortfall, 14 to 37 per cent seed samples
and 13 to 21 per cent fertilizer samples were found substandard as the samples did
not conform to the permissible standards. No pesticide samples were drawn in the
district Dindori during 2005-10. At the exit conference, the department agreed with

3.1.10.2 Use of sub-standard agricultural inputs

In order to increase the agricultural production and productivity, it was important to
ensure that the necessary inputs available for distribution were of better quality.
During test check of records of 13 DDAs, it was noticed that samples of inputs
pertaining to the period 2005-10 were sent to laboratories for testing which were
subsequently found sub-standard (seed :30329.43 quintals and 290 bags, fertilizer:
10774.23 tonnes and 7488 bags and pesticides: 1303.50 litre and 399.70 kg) as
shown in Appendix 3.6. The DDAs issued orders to stop distribution of inputs
whose samples were found sub-standard and SADOs were instructed to report the
status after conducting physical verification. However, the requisite reports from the
SADOs were not received. Thus the “stop the sale” orders were not found
implemented. The purpose of collection and analysis of samples of inputs for
distribution of quality inputs to farmers could not be fulfilled. During the exit
conference, the department agreed with the audit observation and assured to take
action against the officials responsible for distribution of substandard inputs.

3.1.10.3 Non-maintenance of nitrogen, phosphate and potassium ratio

One of the targets of the Eleventh five year Plan was to give special emphasis on
balanced use of nitrogen (N), phosphate (P) and potassium (K) fertilizers to achieve
the consumption ratio of 4:2:1 by the terminal year. Scrutiny of records of Director,
FW&AD revealed that the ratio of consumption of N, P and K during 2007-10 was
11:6:1, 9:6:1 and 10:6:1 respectively and was not in consonance with the target of the
Eleventh Plan. It was observed that potassium was being used less (63 per cent)
while phosphate was used excessively (11 per cent). The Director attributed the
shortfall to less supply of fertilizers by GOI. The reply is not convincing as adequate
attention was not given by the department for balanced application of fertilizer as a
result of which, the envisaged consumption ratio could not maintained. At the exit
conference, the department assured to maintain the prescribed ratio in future.

As per the provisions envisaged in the Departmental Manual, soil survey units were
to conduct surveys and compile their findings and results of soil survey annually.
These compiled findings were required to be apprised to the Director, FW&AD with
special reference to unwarranted transformations and changes being noticed in the
soil series of the State for initiating suitable preventive and remedial action. Eight
soil survey units” were established by the department to provide facilities for soil

3.1.10.5 Soil testing laboratory

Sixteen soil testing labs were run by the department to carry out soil analysis. Audit,
however, observed shortfalls in collection of samples ranging between 30 to 48 per
cent during 2005-10. Four out of 16 soil testing labs in the State were test-checked
during the performance audit. It was noticed that each laboratory was to cover two or
more districts. The targets for drawing soil samples were fixed district-wise by the
Directorate and block-wise by the DDAs but there were shortfalls in collection of
samples ranging between 16 and 64 per cent in the selected districts. The year-wise
targets, achievement and shortfalls in the testing laboratories during 2005-10 are
detailed in Appendix 3.7. The shortfalls were attributed by the Assistant Soil Testing
Officers (ASTOs) to non-collection of soil samples by the extension workers as per
the targets fixed for the districts by Directorate, non-availability of budget allotment
and non-availability of micro testing facilities in the laboratories. Director, FW&AD
assured to post trained staff and to install modern equipments in the laboratories.

3.1.11 Human Resource Management

3.1.11.1 Sanctioned strength and men-in-position

The Directorate, FW&AD did not have the detailed position of staff deployed in its
field units. Against the overall sanctioned strength of 14,218 employees, there were
10,863 men-in-position under Directorate, FW&AD. Out of total shortage of 3,355,
shortage of technical staff was 1917 (DDA-9, ADA-244, SADO-187, ADO-463 and.
RAEO-1014). Against the sanctioned strength of 1,142, there were 801 men-in-
position under the Directorate, Agriculture Engineering. Sanctioned strength and
men-in-position of the department as on March 2010 is shown in Appendix 3.8.
Director, FW&AD replied that the posts of DDAs had been filled up and action to fill
up the vacant post of other staff was under process. At the exit conference, the
department agreed to fill up the vacant posts.

3.1.11.2 Improper deployment of staff

The department had fixed (June 2007) office wise/cadre wise posts by deducting 30
per cent posts pertaining to subordinate offices including the Directorate.
Accordingly, the excess staff was to be transferred so as to make the deployment
logical. Scrutiny of records of 10 out of 13 test-checked districts revealed that the
excess staff were working in various offices and redeployment had not been made by
the department. The expenditure on pay and allowances incurred on 124 excess staff
was & 3.49 crore during 2007-10 as shown in Appendix 3.9. District officers stated
that action would be taken as per instructions of the Directorate. The assessment of
man-power requirement and deployment was not rationale.

3.1.11.3 Irregular attachment of staff

Government in the General Administration Department issued (January 2000)
instructions for termination of the practice of attachment of staff. Scrutiny of records
of seven out of 13 districts revealed that 19 officials were attached to other
departments in nine offices’. Expenditure on pay and allowance incurred on these
staff amounted to हैं 89.61 lakh. Their continued attachment for long periods called
for reassessment of staffrequirement in the aforesaid offices. At the exit conference,
the department assured to issue instructions in this regard.

3.1.11.4 Performance of training centres

Agriculture Extension Training Centres were established in 19 districts for imparting
training to extension staff and farmers. During 2005-10, 16,398 staff were provided
training against the target of 18,554 and 1,20,118 farmers were provided training
against the target of 1,28,292 by 18 out of 19 training centres. In the seven training
centres” of selected districts, it was noticed that (of the total 265 working days in a
year) the centres remained idle for the periods ranging from 16 days (Naugaon during
2007-08) to 265 days (Betul Bazar during 2005-06). The shortfall in imparting
training to staff ranged between eight and 41 per cent and to farmers between 11 and
47 per cent, At the exit conference, the department accepted the audit observation.

3.1.13 Monitoring, vigilance and evaluation

3.1.13.1 Non-revision/updation of manual

The Department of Agriculture was reorganised in January 1965 and the Manual of
the department was published in 1981. The departmental Manual was required to be
revised once in every five years by way of amendments, additions and deletions as
per provision contained in Para 3 XII (87) and 19(X) 768 of manual. Despite changes
in the procedure of implementation of various schemes/activities, organisational
structure, establishment and introduction of new schemes, the Manual had not been
revised/updated. At the exit conference, the department assured to update the
manual.

3.1.13.2 Internal audit

Internal Audit (IA) examines and evaluates compliance to departmental rules and
procedures and statutory provision so as to provide independent assurance to the
management at senior levels on the adequacy of the internal control framework in the
department and its implementation thereof. A separate Internal Audit Wing was
formed under the supervision of Joint Director (Finance) comprising two Audit
Officers and 28 other staff but only two auditors were found working. The roster of
internal audit was not prepared during 2005-06 to 2007-08 and periodicity was not
fixed for conducting the audit. No audit was conducted during 2005-06. During
2006-10, out of 308 offices, 39 offices were audited and compliance report of only 2
offices was received so far. Since all the units could not be covered during the year,
there was a necessity for defining parameters regarding selection of unbiased sample
on the basis of risk perception. Director, FW&AD stated that units were not covered
due to shortage of staff. Internal audit wing was not formed by the Director,
Agriculture Engineering. At the exit conference, the department agreed with the
audit observation.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

3.1.13.3 Departmental inspections

Periodic inspections by departmental officers are an important and effective tool
with the management to ensure proper functioning of the schemes in the department.
No inspection was conducted by the Director, FW&AD during 2005-06 and 2007-
09. Out of 24 offices, only five inspections were carried out during 2006-07 and
2009-10 as per the roster of inspection. Out of 456 inspections prescribed in the
roster for the Additional Directors/Joint Directors, only 73 (16 per cent) inspections
were carried out during 2005-10. Non-conducting of inspections was attributed by
Director, FW&AD to rush of work. Inspections were essential to check and improve
the working of department.

Rule 291 of MPTC-vol-I provides that every DDO should make the monthly
inspection of account of his office and report be submitted to higher authorities. But,
the DDOs did not carry out the regular inspection. At the exit conference, the
department agreed with the audit observation and assured compliance in future.

3.1.13.4 Concurrent evaluation

The National Food Security Mission scheme envisaged baseline surveys and
concurrent evaluation by the Economics and Statistics Department. Similarly, a State
level monitoring committee under the chairmanship of the State Mission Director,
FW&AD with members from the line departments, State Agricultural Universities,
lead banks and the Indian Council of Agriculture Research was to be formed and the
State Economics and Statistics Department was to prepare the prescribed format for
data collection pertaining to different parameters of the Mission for monitoring. It
was observed that neither the survey and concurrent evaluation was done nor was the
committee formed. The formats for data collection were also not prepared.

The Director, FW&AD replied that the work was under process and the report was
yet to be received from the concerned department.

3.1.13.5 District Level Monitoring Committees

> Under the National Agriculture Insurance Scheme guidelines (1999), District
level monitoring committees (under the chairmanship of Collector) were
required to meet fortnightly for effective monitoring and coordination among
different departments on proper implementation of the Crop Insurance
Scheme. It was noticed that the meetings were not held accordingly in the
test-checked districts. The DDA, Dindori did not provide information
regarding the meetings held.

> A district level committee was to be formed under the Chairmanship of Joint
Director, Agriculture for successful implementation of the National Project
on Management of Swasthya Health and Fertility. The committee was to
meet once in every month and submit the reply to Director, FW&AD. In the
test-checked districts, it was noticed that the meetings were not held
accordingly.

118



Chapter-III Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

3.1.13.6 Departmental enquiry and Legal cell in the directorate

Although a departmental enquiry cell was functioning at the Directorate FW&AD
level, 233 cases pertaining to period 2005-10 were pending as of March 2010, 53
court cases were pending for which first replies were still to be submitted by the
department. The reason for non-submission of first replies was not furnished to
Audit. Thirteen pension cases and one family pension case and 224 appointment
cases on compassionate grounds were also pending at the Directorate FW&AD level
during 2005-10. Four public complaints received during 2008-10 and 14 cases
pertaining to economic offence wing and vigilance department were pending. The
teasons for pendency were not furnished to audit.

3.1.13.7 Response to audit

The Principal Accountant General (Civil and Commercial) Madhya Pradesh
conducts statutory audit of the department and its subordinate offices. The
shortcomings are brought to notice through Inspection Reports (IRs). As of
September 2010, 1306 paragraphs of 533 IRs issued up to March 2010 were pending
for settlement. Of these, 126 paragraphs of 71 IRs were outstanding for more than 10
years. This showed lack of response to control mechanisms prescribed for close
monitoring and timely action on audit objections. During the exit conference, the
department agreed to take action to settle the outstanding paragraphs.

3.1.14 Conclusion

Plans for production set in the Annual Plans were not followed. Budgetary and
expenditure controls were weak as reflected from excess provisions of funds made in
both the original and supplementary budgets, rush of expenditure at the close of the
financial year and underutilisation of Central assistance. The targets set in the Tenth
and Eleventh Five Year Plans for production of cereals, oilseeds and pulses were not
achieved. The object of establishing government farms was not achieved due to
underutilisation of cultivable lands, low production and excess expenditure over
tevenue earned. The performance of quality testing laboratories, soil surveys, soil
testing and training was far below the targets. There was no scientific assessment of
manpower requirement and deployment. Internal audit was inadequate and
ineffective. Monitoring was unsatisfactory as prescribed departmental inspections
were not conducted and district level committees were not functional.

3.1.15 Recommendations

> Targets for production should be in consonance with the targets fixed in the
Annual Plan.
> Budgetary and financial control should be improved to ensure preparation

of realistic budget estimates linked to programme implementation.

119



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

The department should draw up a strategy to increase the crop area and
foodgrain production and strengthening soil survey and soil testing
mechanism.

Efforts should be made for timely storage and distribution of quality
agricultural inputs to the farmers.

Functioning of Government agriculture farms should be reviewed and
strengthened to make them commercially viable.

The department should maintain detailed records of beneficiaries in order to
verify the correctness of selection and passing on of actual benefits.

Cadre review should be conducted in order to ensure availability of
prescribed staffin various cadres and offices.

The monitoring mechanism should be strengthened at various levels to
ensure achievement of the objectives of the department.

The department agreed with the recommendations in the exit conference.

Chapter-III Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

Animal Husbandry Department

3.2 Chief Controlling Officer based Audit of Animal Husbandry
Department

Highlights

The Animal Husbandry Department is responsible for all-round development of
livestock wealth in the State. The Director, Veterinary Services is the Chief
Controlling Officer (CCO) of the department. CCO based audit of the department
revealed inadequate planning, unnecessary augmentation of supplementary
provisions, drawal of funds in anticipation of requirement and diversion of funds.
There was shortfall in artificial insemination, calving and distribution of
livestock. Veterinary farms were running in losses. The department did not achieve
its objectives fully due to shortfalls in infrastructure, human resources and
inadequate internal control.

Some of the important findings noticed during audit are as follows:

The Annual Plans were prepared on ad hoc basis. The financial management
lacked foresight leading to unnecessary supplementary provisions, surrenders,
parking of funds, diversion of funds and rush of expenditure in the month of
March.

(Paragraphs 3.2.6.1, 3.2.7.1, 3.2.7.2, 3.2.7.3 and 3.2.7.4)

The achievement of artificial insemination fell short by 13 to 18 per cent while
calving through artificial insemination fell short by eight to 23 per cent.
Veterinary farms incurred losses of = 14.22 crore during 2007-10. Livestock
distribution fell short of targets. Production of eggs and wool declined heavily.

(Paragraphs 3.2.8.2, 3.2.8.4, 3.2.8.6 and 3.2.8.7)

As against the requirement of 5,813 veterinary centres, only 2,346 centres
existed. Out of 4,582 institutions, 1,180 institutions were functioning without
their own buildings. A total of 147 hospitals were functioning without
Veterinary Assistant Surgeons.

(Paragraphs 3.2.9.1, 3.2.9.2 and 3.2.9.3)

The Milk Quality Control laboratory was not established and the State Disease
Diagnostic laboratory was not upgraded.

(Paragraphs 3.2.10.1 and 3.2.10.3)

There was shortage of veterinary staff ranging between 17 to 41 per cent and
shortfall between 45 to 72 per cent in imparting training to Veterinary Assistant
Surgeons and Assistant Veterinary Field Officers regarding artificial
insemination.

(Paragraphs 3.2.11.1 and 3.2.11.4)

121



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Stores and stock accounts were not prepared by 25 out of 30 test- checked units.
Hence, annual physical verification was not possible. Unserviceable items
worth 1.30 crore were lying undisposed of for periods upto 20 years.

(Paragraphs 3.2.12.1 and 3.2.12.2)

Vaccines were being produced by the Institute of Animal Health and Veterinary
Biologicals, without licences since 2002. Vaccines costing = 1.78 crore were not
preserved at the required temperatures.

(Paragraphs 3.2.12.4 and 3.2.12.6)

3.2.1 Introduction

The Animal Husbandry Department (Department) is responsible for all-round
development of livestock wealth in the State. The Director, Veterinary Services
(Director) is the Chief Controlling Officer (CCO) in the department and is
responsible for planning the annual activities of the department for implementation
of various schemes and programmes through appropriate controls, monitoring and
evaluations. The main objectives of the department are to provide:

> Disease-free, low cost and nutritious animal protein for the human population
through animal health care and breed improvement;

> Protection to the human population from zoonotic diseases through curative
as well as prophylactic measures;

> Employment opportunities to weaker sections of the society through
extension services.

Major components of the departmental activities consist of animal health care, breed.
improvement, preservation and development of superior indigenous germ plasm,
management of veterinary centres and animal care including control of anima
diseases, management of laboratory services and employment generation through
investment in animal husbandry.

The department is also responsible for enforcement of provisions of seven State an
four Central Acts, orders and rules framed thereunder. (Details in Appendix-3.10).
According to the Animal Census 2007, the total livestock, poultry and other anima
population in the State was 4.89 crore. This included 2.19 crore cattle, 91.29 lakh
buffaloes, 3.90 lakh sheep, 90.14 lakh goats, 0.27 lakh horses, 73.84 lakh poultry and.
10.21 lakh other animals. The department implemented 39 schemes (32 State
Schemes, five Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS) and two Central Sector Schemes
(CS). One CSS was fully funded by Government of India (GOD) and two CSSs were
funded on 50:50 basis by the GOI and the Government. Cost sharing of two CSSs
were 75:25 and 80:20 between GOI and Government. Two CSs were also fully
funded by the GOL. (Details in Appendix-3.11).

122



Chapter-III Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

3.2.2 Organisational Set-up

The department is headed by the Principal Secretary at the State level and is
responsible for implementation of Government policies/programmes/ schemes.
Overall financial /administrative/ technical control is vested with the Director
Veterinary Services (Director) as field officer. The Director is assisted by Joint
Directors (JDs) and Deputy Directors (DDs) at headquarters and nine JDs at the
divisional level. At the district level, animal health is taken care of by 50 DDs, who
are assisted by Veterinary Assistant Surgeons (VASs) posted in Veterinary Hospitals
(608). The VAS also look after 2,236 Artificial Insemination (AI) centres, 22 Disease
Investigation Laboratories (DILs), 26 Mobile Units (MUs) and 26 Ambulatory
Clinics (ACs) and are assisted by Assistant Veterinary Field Officers (AVFOs)
posted in these units.

3.2.3 Audit Objectives

The audit objectives were to ascertain whether the:

> planning and programme management were efficient and effective;

> budgetary control and financial management wereadequate;

> effective schemes/projects were implemented timely and monitored
properly;

> human resources management and review of staff requirements were proper;

> internal controls, monitoring and evaluation of schemes/programmes were
adequate.

3.2.4 Audit Criteria

The audit criteria included the following:

> Departmental manuals for planning and implementation of
schemes/programmes;

> Budget Manual, Madhya Pradesh Financial Code (MPFC) and Madhya
Pradesh Treasury Code (MPTC);

Vv

Government rules, notifications, guidelines and instructions issued by the
State and Central Government;

> Procedures prescribed for monitoring and evaluation of schemes.

3.2.5 Scope and Methodology of Audit

Out of 103 Drawing and Disbursing Officers (DDOs) in the department, records of
30 DDOs including the Director, Veterinary Services, Director, Institute of Animal

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Directors (DDs) including their field formations, two Government farms and one

adopting the Simple Random Sample Without Replacement (SRSWOR) method. An

April 2010, wherein the objectives, criteria and scopes of audit were discussed. The
exit conference was held on 10 December 2010 with the Principal Secretary and the
teplies of the department have been incorporated at appropriate places.

Audit Observations

3.2.6 Planning

3.2.6.1 Planning

The department was required to prepare a Perspective Plan (Five Year Plan) for
achieving its mandate. Further, the Perspective Plan was to be broken into actionable
Annual Plans by fixing suitable targets for each year. Action Plans for each year were
to be prepared by the Directorate, consolidating district level Plans to enable
interventions in the veterinary sector.

Scrutiny of records of the Directorate and selected units revealed that the Action
Plans were prepared on ad hoc basis without analysing reasons for shortfalls in
achievement of targets and assessment of scheme-wise/ component-wise needs vis-
a-vis available resources, infrastructure and other facilities.

A comprehensive database in respect of requirement of manpower, materials and
survey of schemes was also not available in the department to facilitate preparation
of the Perspective and Annual Plans.

The Director stated (August 2010) that Annual and Five Year Action Plans were
prepared and approved by the District Planning Committee (DPC) and the State
Planning Committee (SPC). However, the surveys and assessments of shortfalls in
veterinary fields at the block and villages level were not done by the department
before preparation of Annual and Five Year Plans.

The reply is not acceptable as the Plans were based on ad hoc increases in previous
targets without assessment of the actual requirements from the grass root level and
the resources available. The survey reports and comprehensive data base were also
not available for the preparation of Plans.

Chapter-III Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

3.2.6.2 State Livestock Breeding Policy

Numbers of The State Livestock Breeding Policy for Cattle and Buffalo envisaged that exotic
animals breed levels should be maintained at the level of 50 per cent in general. The breeding
मणि policy also envisaged genetic upgradation of indigenous cattle and buffalos and
among various increase in production of milk and other livestock produce. Artificial Insemination
species. (AI) and Natural Insemination (NI) were the main activity to achieve breed

improvement in the State.

As per the 17° Animal Census of 2003, there were 3.17 lakh cross-breed cattle (1.68
per cent of total 1.89 crore cattle) which increased to 4.75 lakh (2.17 per cent) of the
total 2.19 crore cattle population in the State (18" Animal Census 2007).

The position of cross-breed buffalos was not made available by the department. The
department had also not provided data of breed improvement in case of sheep and
goats. There was a 16 to 87 per cent decline in numbers noticed in animals during 18"
Animal Census as detailed in Table 3.12 below.

(iti) since the actual expenditure of 783.77 crore under the revenue section was

> in three cases, there was no expenditure made against allotments of = 15.35

3.2.7.2 Parking of funds in Personal Deposit and Civil Deposit account

Subsidiary Rule 284 of MPTC prohibits drawal of funds from the treasury unless
required for immediate disbursement. It was noticed that contrary to the above rule,
Central assistance of 60.18 crore (® 46.78 crore by the Director and® 13.40 crore by
the Director, Institute of Animal Health and Veterinary Biologicals, Indore) under
various schemes was drawn from the treasury during 2007-10 without immediate
tequirement and kept in the Personal Deposit (PD)/Civil Deposit (CD) account.
Balances lying in these accounts as on 31 March 2010 pertaining to year 2007-10
were as detailed in Appendix- 3.14.

On this being pointed out, the Director stated (August 2010) that the funds were
drawn and deposited in PD/CD accounts to avoid lapse of budget grants and efforts
were being made for its utilisation. The reply indicates that the funds were drawn
without any immediate requirement in contravention of the provisions of Rule 284 of
MPTC. The Principal Secretary, during the exit conference, stated that consultant
advice was necessary in planning of IAHVB works.

3.2.7.3 Diversion of funds

An amount of € 5.28 crore was sanctioned (March 2009) by GOI under the Rashtriya
Krishi Vikas Yojna (RKVY) scheme for maintenance of cold chain/expansion of
immunisation. The Director procured cryocanes™ costing %1.17 crore for
preservation/transportation of frozen semen for artificial insemination from the
above sanction. Cryocanes had no correlation with maintenance of cold
chain/expansion ofimmunisation.

On this being pointed out, the Director admitted (August 2010) that cryocanes were
procured for making semen available to districts. The reply was not acceptable as
cryocane was not a device prescribed for transportation of vaccines.

3.2.7.4 Rush of Expenditure

As per Rules 56 (3) and 69 of GFRs, expenditure should uniformly be spent
throughout the year as far as possible. Rush of expenditure at the close of financial
year is prone to the risk of Government not getting proper value for money as the
expenditure taking place without due diligence and care. Appropriation audit
revealed that during 2007-10, the percentage of expenditure in the month of March
remained between 14 and 19 per cent as shown in Table 3.14.

3.2.7.10 Unadjusted temporary advances

According to Rule 53(IV) of MPTC, temporary advances should not be allowed to
remain unadjusted for more than three months. Scrutiny of records in test-checked
units revealed that in 13 units, temporary advances of € 38.65 lakh” were pending for
adjustment for periods ranging from six months to 30 years. The DDs stated (May
2010) that efforts were being made to adjust temporary advances.

Further scrutiny of cash books of the DDs Narsinghpur and Hoshangabad, revealed
that amounts of € 4.11 lakh and हैं 0.07 lakh respectively were being shown as
advances in the cash book of DDs from 1989 and 1979 respectively due to non-
availability of details of advances and persons to whom these advances were paid.

During the exit conference, the department assured to take necessary action for
adjustment of advances.

3.2.7.1] Defective maintenance of Cash Book

Subsidiary Rule 53 of MPTC, provides that fortnightly verification of drawals is to
be conducted with reference to treasury voucher slips. Temporary advances are
tequired to be recorded with red ink and not to be accounted for as final payments. At
the end of each month, the DDO is required to verify cash under his dated signature
and the analysis of cash is to be recorded in the cash book. Daily totals of cash book
are required to be checked by a person other than the writer of cash book and each
DDO is required to maintain a cash book in the form MPTC-S. Every transaction as
soon as it occurs is required to be entered in the cash book and got attested by the
competent authority.

3.2.7.12 Defects in maintenance of Bill Register

According to Subsidiary Rule 197 of MPTC, the bill register should be reviewed
monthly by DDO and results recorded in it. Monthly abstracts should be prepared at
the end of the month giving details of bills submitted to the treasury, the total
numbers of bills passed, the total numbers of cancelled bills, bills pending etc.

Scrutiny of bill registers in 137 test-checked units revealed that neither was the
teview of bill registers conducted by DDOs nor were abstracts prepared. DDOs
assured (May to September 2010) its compliance in future.

3.2.8 Programme management

3.2.8.1 Non-conducting of inspection of bulls

The Madhya Pradesh Livestock Improvement Act 1950 (MPLSIA) stipulated that no
person was to keep in his possession any bull without marks for reproduction
purpose. The Veterinary Officer (VO) was responsible for inspection of bulls in the
area under jurisdiction. He was either to mark the bulls for reproduction purpose or
order their castration.

Audit observed that compliance of the provisions of MPLSIA was not on record in
any test-checked unit as records related to inspection of bulls and marking of bulls
were not maintained in the veterinary units of the districts. During the exit
conference, the department assured to take action for implementation of the Acts and
Rules.

services, The breedable population of 1.21 crore (as per Animal Census 2007) of the
State was to be covered forinsemination in the ratio of AI 60 percent and NI 40 percent.

3.2.8.5 Non-recovery of Artificial Insemination fee at enhance rates

Government fixed (January 2005) AI fee of € 30 per AI for urban areas and % 20 per
AI for rural areas. In the test-checked units, it was found that AI fees were not charged.
at the prescribed rates. Instead, the districts were charging fees at different rates,
which resulted ina loss of revenue amounting to% 1.70 crore” during 2007-10.

At the exit conference, the Principal Secretary assured that fees would strictly be
charged as per the prescribed rates.

3.2.8.6 Beneficiary-oriented Schemes

Beneficiary-oriented schemes viz. Mass Poultry Production Programme (Backyard)
and distribution of bulls, goats, milch animals etc. and setting up of dairy and
poultry units at small scales on subsidy basis were being implemented for generating
employment among weaker sections of the society. Subsidy plus beneficiaries shares
were deposited with various agencies like the Madhya Pradesh Pashudhan evam

In the test-checked districts, the production of milk declined in eight districts,

districts ranging from nine to 63 per cent and wool production declined in six
districts ranging from eight to 100 per cent during 2008-09. District-wise data for
2009-10 was not available in the Directorate. The Director stated (August 2010) that
shortfall in the production of eggs was due to the impact of bird flu in the State.
Shortfall in wool production was due to reduction in the number of sheep in the State
and the fact that sheep-owners were not taking interest in sheep-rearing.

The reply is not acceptable as the department neither furnished any documentary
evidence of incidence of bird flu nor any departmental/State Disease Diagnostic
Laboratory (SDDL) report of such incidence was on record of test-checked units.
The department did not conduct any survey to assess the reasons for sheep-owners
not taking interest in sheep-rearing. Thus, the department failed to take remedial
measures to encourage sheep-rearing and increase in wool production.

3.2.8.8 Non-examination of agriculture cattle for slaughter

According to the Agriculture Cattle Preservation Act 1959 (ACPA) a Veterinary
Officer (VO) was required to be posted for examination of agricultural cattle before
slaughter. A register in form C was to be maintained by him showing details of cattle
examined and certificates under the provisions laid down in the Act was to be issued
by him. However, VOs were neither posted for implementation of ACPA nor were
registers maintained in form C.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

3.2.9 Capacity building

Shortage of

3.2.9.1 Shortage of Veterinary Centres

As per the recommendations of the National Agriculture Council (NAC), one
veterinary centre (either veterinary hospital or dispensary) was required for 7000
livestock population. Similarly, one AI centre was required for one thousand
livestock breedable population. Scrutiny of records revealed that against the
requirement of 5,813 veterinary centres for the existing 4.07 crore livestock
population in the State, 2,346 veterinary centres (608 hospitals and 1,738
dispensaries) were functioning. Thus, 3,467 veterinary centres were short in the
State. Similarly, against the requirement of 12,104 AI centres for 1.21 crore
breedable population, only 2,236 AI centres (18 per cent) were functioning.

Shortage of veterinary centres and AI centres adversely affected breed improvement
and the productivity ofanimals.

3.2.9.2 Inadequate physical infrastructure for Veterinary Services

Greater access and proper implementation of various services cannot be ensured
without sufficient infrastructure. Scrutiny of records of the Directorate revealed that
out of total 4,582 institutions, 1,180 (26 per cent) institutions were functioning
without their own buildings. In the test-checked units, 572 veterinary institutions
were functioning without their own buildings, 325 units were without electricity and
311 were without cold chain facilities.

On this being pointed out, the Director/DDs stated (August 2010) that efforts were
being made to increase the infrastructure.

3.2.9.3 Hospital and Dispensaries functioning without VASs and AV FOs

With a view to provide veterinary care to animals, veterinary hospitals and
dispensaries were established. According to the staff pattern, one Veterinary
Assistant Surgeon (VAS) for each hospital and one Assistant Veterinary Field Officer
(AVFO) for each dispensary along with para-veterinary staff were sanctioned for
providing veterinary care in health institutions. Scrutiny of records revealed that 147
hospitals were functioning without VAS in the State. Out of 20 test-checked districts,
inthe 11 districts, 64 hospitals and 141 dispensaries were functioning without VASs
and AVFOs for one to 14 years. (Appendix-3.16)

On this being pointed out, the Director/DDs stated (August 2010) that VASs and
AVFOs of neighbour hospitals were diverted for providing treatment at the vaccant
hospitals and dispensaries and action for posting of VASs/AVFOs was being
initiated. During the exit conference, the Principal Secretary intimated that
recruitment of 89 personnel was under process.

3.2.9.4 Irregular expenditure under Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojna

The State Level Sanctioning Committee (SLSC) constituted (July 2008) under the
chairmanship of the Chief Secretary to consider the departmental project report
under the State Agriculture Plan (SAP) for Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojna (RKVY)
instructed (July 2008) that activities such as purchase of vehicles, posting of
permanent staff, construction of permanent structures and large buildings were not
permissible under RKVY.

Scrutiny of records revealed that the Director purchased (December 2008) three
Mahindra Bolero vehicles (¥ 12.87 lakh) and two tractors 8.91 lakh) despite
prohibition by SLSC and these were allotted to farms. Thus there was an irregular
expenditure of € 21.78 lakh.

Scrutiny also revealed that funds amounting to हैं 12.35 crore were released
(November 2008 to August 2009) for construction of a State level training centre at
Bhopal and cattle sheds in veterinary farms. The construction work of the training
centre was still incomplete (August 2010) and furniture, laptops and computers
worth हैं 21.90 lakh were purchased during 2008-09 and were lying unused in the
directorate. Thus release of € 12.35 crore was irregular.

The above facts indicate that the expenditure of f 12.78 crore was incurred without
observing the instructions of SLSC. During the exit conference, the department
assured to get sanction from SLSC.

3.2.10 Quality Control

3.2.10.1 Milk and Milk Products Quality Control Laboratory

A mention was made in para 5.1.8.5 of the Audit Report of 2005-06 that out of an
amount of € 32.90 lakh released by GOI in October 2001 for establishment of a
Quality Control Laboratory (QCL) at Bhopal, an amount of 8.04 lakh was spent on
purchase of laboratory items. An amount of = 24.86 lakh was refunded to GOI in
March 2007 due to non-revalidation of sanction. In addition to this, an amount of 35
lakh was received in 2009-10 from GOI which also could not be utilised till October
2010 due to non-construction of QCL building. Thus, the QCL was not established
despite availability of funds. The expenditure of = 8.04 lakh on purchase of
laboratory items also remained unfruitful as these items could not be installed due to
non-establishment of QCL. During the exit conference, the Principal Secretary
stated that survey was being conducted to finalise the design of the QCL.

3.2.10.2 Upgradation of Feed Testing Laboratory

Estimation of urea, alfa toxin and minerals in animal feed is essential because these
components increase the crude protein percentage in livestock/poultry feed which
causes poor health status and death of animals. A feed testing laboratory was
established in 1972-73 at Bhopal. The laboratory had analysis facility of only crude
protein, crude fibre, crude fat and percentage of insoluble minerals like calcium and

Government of India sanctioned and provided (2008-09) an amount of € 22 lakh for
upgradation of the State Disease Diagnostic Laboratory (SDDL) Bhopal as a Bio

Preparedness Control and Containment of Avian Influenza.

of laboratory building and हैं 1.50 lakh to SDDL Bhopal). Out of & 12.50 lakh

₹ 9.91 lakh was deposited in a PD account. Thus upgradation of laboratory could not

would be purchased after approval of tender.

of Foot and Mouth Disease (FMD), Haemorrhagic Septicaemia (HS) Black Quarter

3.2.10.5  Non-establishment of quarantine stations

The Central Province Cattle Disease Act 1934 (CPCDA)/ The Prevention and
Control of Infectious and Contagious Diseases in Animals Act, 2009 required
establishment of a quarantine station at the entryway of the State/districts. The VO
was to be posted for checking migratory cattle to check the contagious diseases. He
was empowered to detain cattle for vaccination etc. or allow them to pass out from
quarantine check post and issued permits under Section 7 of the Act. However,
quarantine stations were not established in any of the test-checked units in the State
under CPCDA.

Scrutiny of records of the DD concerned revealed that payment of the salary of four
staff members continued to be paid from the Animal Husbandry Department even
after 2000. This resulted in irregular expenditure of 43.93 lakh on the staffof the farm.

3.2.12 Stores and Material Management

3.2.12.1 Non-preparation of Stores and Stock accounts

Rules 124 to 126 of MPFC provide that stores and stock accounts should be prepared
annually by each office dealing with purchase and issue of medicines, equipment and.
machineries. Physical verification of stores should also be conducted annually and
the store-keeper should be trained in the inventory control mechanism. Test check of
records of the Directorate and selected units revealed that stores and stock accounts
were prepared only in five units viz. Balaghat, Betul, Chhatarpur, Hoshangabad and.
Indore (IAHVB) out of the 30 test-checked units.

DDs of the other units stated (July 2010) that stores and stock accounts would be
prepared in future.

3.2.12.2 Disposal of unserviceable items

According to Rule 141 of MPFC, unserviceable items should be identified during
annual physical verification and disposed off through auction etc. and should be
finally written off from the stores accounts.

Scrutiny of records in selected units revealed that unserviceable items such as
Nitrogen plant, Crayocanes, Tractors, Vehicles etc. valuing हैं 1.30 crore were lying
inthe stores for periods up to 20 years.

The Director/DDs stated (June 2010) that the disposal was pending due to fixing of
higher prices than fixed by the department for auction. Efforts made to revise the
prices were also not available on record.

3.2.12.3 Lack of quality assurance of medicines

Medicines required in the department were being purchased from firms after inviting
tenders and supplied to the veterinary centres for utilisation. There was no system in
place for checking of medicines before use and therefore, no quality checks were
applied before use of the medicines. All the medicines purchased were being
consumed without ascertaining their quality.

During the exit conference, the department assured to take necessary action for
quality control of medicines.
