3.2.12.4 Production of vaccines without renewal of drug manufacturing licence

The Institute of Animal Health and Veterinary Biologicals (AHVB) was established
with the objective of large scale production of veterinary biological and other
ancillary preparations as per prescribed standards. For this, the institute required a
drug manufacturing licence which was to be renewed from the Controller, Food and
Drugs (CFD).

During scrutiny of records, it was observed that renewal of the drugs licence of this
institute was pending from 2002 due to non-compliance of requirements as per the
current Good Manufacturing Practices (CGMP), Rules. As reported (February 2003)
by CFD, the institute was engaged in manufacturing various vaccines without valid
required licences.

Government sanctioned (2007-08) an amount of हैं 10 crore (हैं eight crore for
construction and % two crore for purchase of machines and equipment) for
implementation of cGMP norms. No upgradation work was taken up and the funds
were deposited in PD accounts. On this being pointed out, the Director stated
(September 2010) that modernisation of the institute as per requirement of cGMP
would be completed after appointing a consultant at the Government level.

3.2.12.5 Use of animal feed without testing

According to instructions issued by the Director, Veterinary Services and also by
Feed Analysis Laboratory (FAL) Bhopal, feed of all the batch numbers purchased by
the institutions was to be got analysed from the feed testing laboratory. Feed, the
sample of which was found sub-standard was not to be used for animals.

Scrutiny of records of DDs revealed that feed valuing = 90.78 lakh” purchased under
various departmental schemes was utilised without getting sample tested in any
Government laboratory and no collateral study was found in the department on the
effect of this feed.

On this being pointed out, the Director and DDs stated (September 2010) that the
feed was purchased from Government undertakings/firms and hence was not tested.
The reply is not acceptable because samples of all the feed purchased were required.
tobe tested.

3.2.12.6 Preservation and transportation of vaccines at higher temperature

It was provided in the brochure designed by the Director, IAHVB Indore that while
supplying vaccines, they were required to be preserved between temperatures of 2°C
to 8°C.

Scrutiny of records of test-checked units revealed that vaccines costing हैं 1.78 crore
were not preserved at the required temperatures to save their potency due to non-
availability of deep freezers, refrigerator van (used for maintaining cold chain).
Thus, their storage and transportation at atmospheric temperature would have been
adverse effect on their potency.

On this being pointed out, the DDs replied (May to September 2010) that the
vaccines were stored in cool places and utilised without testing due to lack of testing
facilities. The reply is not tenable as storage at atmospheric temperature was bound
to affect the potency of the vaccines.

3.2,12.7 Excess expenditure due to wastage of Straws

Straw were required for AI and Liquid Nitrogen (LN,) was required for preservation
of straws. One straw dose was sufficient for one AI.

Scrutiny of records of test-checked DDs revealed that six to 19 per cent straw doses
were wasted during the process of AI due to non-availability of trained staff and
equipment, resulting in extra expenditure of € 23.45 lakh (% 11.26 lakh on the cost of
straws and® 12.19 lakh on cost of proportionate LN,) showing unit to unit variations
in wastage of straws. Further, it was found that the department had not conducted any
study to fix the norms of wastage of straws during the process of AI. Similarly, in the
test-checked units, variations were found in the cost of preservation of straws
ranging from 7.75 to 21.88 per straw dose. (Appendix -3.17).

On this being pointed out, the DDs replied (June 2010) that this wastage was
procedural. In the exit conference, the Principal Secretary stated that a committee
would be constituted to study and suggest the norms of wastage of straws.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

3.2.13 Internal Controls

Internal audit is an important tool for the Management to ascertain the accuracy and
effectiveness of the internal control mechanism.

3.2.13.1 Departmental Manuals

No Departmental Manual was available in the Directorate as well as in the test-
checked units. The Directorate stated (January 2010) that the work of updation of
manual was in progress. The old manual was not shown to Audit.

3.2.13.2 Internal Audit Wing

An Internal Audit Wing (IAW) had been set up at the Directorate level. There were no
manuals/codes governing internal audit. In the IAW, out of 18 sanctioned posts, only
three men (one Audit Officer and two Sub Auditors) were in position (August 2010).

Only seven units were audited by the [AW during 2007-08 to 2009-10 against the
targeted 75 units (25 units per year). Thus, internal audit was not effective in the
department.

3.2.13.3 Status of Information technology in the department

Computerisation with internet facilities was made available from the Directorate to
the district headquarters. A departmental website (www.mpdah.gov.in) was created.
under the directions of the State Government and was being utilised for publishing
departmental orders, circulars and other important information. Establishment of a
local area network (LAN) at the Directorate level was not done. In the absence of
LAN and requisite software, the computers could not be used for sharing of IT
resources such as hardware and internet facilities. On this being pointed out, the
Director stated (August 2010) that efforts were being made for establishment of
networking in the department.

3.2.14 Monitoring

There was no centralised database of schemes and programmes for monitoring. As a
result, monitoring at the CCO/ Director level was on ad hoc basis.

3.2.14.1 Department Inspection

Periodical inspections by departmental officers are important and effective tools to
ensure proper functioning of the schemes in the department. As per the instructions
issued by the director in August 1999, all Divisional JDs and DDs are required to

144



There were 238
Inspection
Reports
containing 691
paras pending
for disposal.

Chapter-III Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

submit annual inspection programme together with inspection roster and checklist to
the Directorate. Earlier instructions issued in January 1991 prescribed annual visit to
farms, veterinary hospitals, AI centres and training centres by JDs and DDs. During
test check, no details of departmental inspections were made available by the
JDs/DDs except for the JD, Gwalior division. Shortfall in inspection at the level of
the Director during 2007-10 was 53 to 87 per cent. Further, inspection reports, if any,
and their compliance were not available in any test-checked unit except Gwalior.

Subsidiary Rule 291 of MPTC, stipulates that every JD/DD should undertake
monthly inspection of accounts of his office as a DDO and submit the report to his
higher authority. However, none of the JDs/DDs carried out regular inspections.

3.2.14.2 Departmental enquiry/Public complaints/Court cases

The General Administration Department prescribed time limits for various
departmental proceedings (1997), but there were 30 cases (1994-2010) pending in
the department, out of which in 10 cases, enquiry officers had not been appointed.
upto October 2010. Reasons for delay in completion of departmental enquiries were
not intimated to Audit.

A public complaint cell was functioning at the Directorate level. One JD and two
other members are working in the cell. The cell did not maintain basic records like
complaint receipt register and disposal of these complaints.

Although a legal cell was functioning at the directorate level, 58 cases were pending
for which first replies were still to be submitted by the department. Further, court
orders were received in 200 cases of which compliance of court orders was pending
in 125 cases. Reasons for non-compliance were not furnished to Audit.

3.2.14.3 Outstanding observations of previous audits

As per Regulations on Audit and Accounts framed bythe Comptroller and Auditor
General of India (as notified in the Gazette of India on November 20, 2007) Heads
of Offices andnext higher authorities are required to comply with the observations
contained in the Inspection Reports (IRs), rectify the defects/omissions promptly
and report their compliance to the Principal Accountant General. However, as on
June 2010, 238 IRs involving 691 paras, were pending as detailed in Table 3.23
below:

145



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Total 238 691
(Source: - Records of PAG (Audit)
Lack of response to audit observations depicted lack of a proper accountability
mechanism on the part of the department.

3.2.15 Conclusion

Survey reports and a comprehensive database were not available for planning.
Assessment of the performance of the department revealed that budgetary control
was inadequate, leading to unwarranted supplementary provisions and non-
surrender of savings. Targets set for artificial insemination and natural insemination
were not achieved. Shortfalls in production of eggs and wool were noticed.
Livestock and veterinary farms were incurring losses. The district authorities of test-
checked districts were not aware of the outbreaks of diseases and the number of
animals affected by them. Assessment of requirement of human resources and proper
placement of available manpower was not carried out. No quality assurance
measures were adopted by the department in respect of medicines, vaccines and feed.

3.2.16 Recommendations

> Databases of livestock should be maintained in order to prepare accurate
Annual Plans.

> Budgetary controls should be enforced more effectively.

> Efforts should be made to make all livestock breeding/veterinary farms

economically viable.

> Infrastructural facilities such as buildings for veterinary centres and
laboratories with vaccines and semen storage equipment should be
strengthened.

> Quality testing laboratories should be established /upgraded on priority
basis.

146



Chapter-III Integrated Audit and Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit

> Efforts should be made for timely dissemination of incidence of outbreaks of
diseases by the State Disease Diagnostic Laboratory in order to control
diseases.

> Shortage of veterinary staff should be addressed on priority basis.

> The monitoring mechanism should be made more effective and the Internal
Audit Wing should be strengthened by deploying adequate staff.

Gwalior (B.R. KHAIRNAR)

The Principal Accountant General

(Civil and Commercial Audit)
Madhya Pradesh
Countersigned
New Delhi (VINOD RAD
The Comptroller and Auditor General of India

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Appendix 1.5
(Reference: Paragraph 1.2.8.1, Page 21)

Summary of some important observations of the review committee

Sihawal Main Canal (Length 75.27 km): The work between km 15.24 to km 22.49 was
incomplete and less than the designed section, yet the bills of the contractors were finalised for
which a departmental enquiry be instituted with in aby the CE. The work between km 22 to km 75
was not found as per specifications. The substandard work should be rejected and balance work of
this group was to be completed atthe risk and cost of the contractor.

Keoti Main Canal (Length 90 km): The canal between km 0 to km 8.20 was complete. The work
between km 8.20 to km 32.75 was incomplete and the tenders for leftover work were to be invited at
the risk and cost of the contractor. The work between km 32.75 to km 61.62 was not as per design.
The work between km 61.62 to km 90 was in progress.

Purwa Main Canal (Length 128.28 km): The work from km 13 to km 46.17 and km 68.22 to km
84 was below specifications and was not as per design section. From km 84 to km 122 the work was
in progress and for the work ofkm 122 to km 128 tenders were yet to be invited.

Statement showing details of selection of basins and divisions

Basis of selection of Basins: - Two basins (strata) viz Yamuna and Chambal , out of the
five river basins were selected due to high risk factor and these basins covers three major

projects.

Basis of selection of Divisions:-As per stratified random sampling Random number were

selected

two

taking in to account the risk factors/expenditure/schemes being executed, selected from the
